COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER. MAINTENANCE OF LIVE STOCK

ACT, 1915

MAINTENANCE OF 'LIVE STOCK ORDER, 1915.

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that the Board of Agriculture and Fisheries have by virtue of , and in exercise of the powers vested in them under the above, Act, and for the purpose of maintaining a sufficient Stock of Cattle and Swine, made an Order, to come into operation on the 23rd day of August, 1915, to the following effect:—.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0002.txt | included=true | included =====
PROHIBITION OF SLAUGHTER.

Prohibiting,with certain exceptions, the slaughter of 1. Animals which are visibly or obviously in- calf or in-pig. 2. After the 30th September next all calves in• whih the first permanent molar or grinder tooth is not cut and visible, which for practical purposes means calves six months old, and • 3. tntil the 30th September next, calves un- , der the age of eight weeks. EXCEPTIONS FROM RESTRICTIONS.

The folling exceptionS ,T _9Q allowed by the Order:— -r ow • - (a) Slaughter dan animal under the powers conferred by. the Diseases of Animals Acts, 1894 to 3914, or any Order made thereunder. (b) Slaughter of an animal if necessary or desirable on account of accidental injury to the animal or its illness. (c) Slaughter of an animal if desirable for an exceptional reason or purpose, and if the slaughter is licensed by the Board. (d) Slaughter of a Ralf of a cow of Channel Island, Ayrshire, Kerry, or Dexter breed, and (e) Slaughter of a calf which has been offered for sale by auction at any market or sale yard in England, Wales, or Scotland without reserve, or subject to a reserve price of thirty shillings or less, and for which no bid exceeding thirty. shillings has been made on such occasion; but this provision will have effect only : (1) If the calf so offered for sale is before movement from the market or sale yard marked with a broad arrow by, or under the direction of, the auctioneer in the manner prescribed by the Order, and (2) If such mark is on the calf at the time of slaughter. REVOCATION OF EXISTING ORDER. The Slaughter of Animals Order, 1915, shall Cease to operate as and from the 23rd day of August, 1915.

REGINALD POTTS, Clerk to the County Council. County Offices, Nortligate-street, Chester, • 23rd August, 1915.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST,

Her Little Joke.

In a district in which I spent a fey.- years is I a big rock strangely named by the natives "Churn Milk Joan." When I ,read in my " Examiner " that -two Warringtonians had refused to fill up registration forms, I had just heard from a former colleague in the locality referred to. At one house at which he Called he was told there was a woman higher up the hill who would on no account make the re- quired return. "Oh, indeedl" said he; 4‘ we'll see about that:. The re,c,r,ulations pro- "vide for such cases, and she can be dealt with. Will you favour me with her name?" "Cer- tainly," laughed the buxom dame with whom he chatted; "she's called Churn Milk Joan.' " Unlicensed.

In that same region there was a policeman who, if he could know of this incident, would say, "And serve the blighter jolly well right.". For my friend once took him aside and con- fided to him that at a certain house which he indicated there were two quite old dogs for which no licences had been obtained, and when the constable proceeded to investigate he dis- covered that the canines in question consisted of old-fashioned chimney-piece crockery! The poor little joke disgusted and alienated that police officer—who had wasted anticipatory thanks on the informant. Another Trick.

In mentioning the substitution of the har- monium for the barrel organ, Mr. Arthur Monnfield describes it as an innocent Ettle kist o' whustles " that could play " an in- definite number of tunes." The phrase re- minded me of an advertisethent offer of a wonderful instrument which, although costing only half-a-crown, was guaranteed to play several tunes, the purchaser having the privilege of selection from a lengthy list. An indignant townsman brought the' one, and complained that the misleading " ad." had " had " him. "It's nothing but a sixpenny mouth organ," he exclaimed; "reading the advertisement you'd think it was a musical box, and here it's a simple thing to be blown." Evidently that was the advertiser's estimate .of the great B.P.—that it was a simple thing to be "blown." Parish Clerks and Epitaphs.

John Hopkins, the "epitaph specialist" quoted by Mr., Mounfield, dropped into Latin in the specimen given in his advertisement. The reference brought to my mind one of many quaint epitaphs to be seen in the churchyard of St. Alban's, one of the oldest religious foundations in the City of London (it dates back to the Saxon period). Here it

lc jacet Tom Short-Hose, Sine tombe, sine sheete, sine riches; Qui vixit sine gowne, Sine cloake, sine shirt, sine breeches. Coming more particularly to Parish clerks,. the following is to be seen in Selby Church- yard: Here lies the body of poor Frank How, Parish Clerk and grave-stone cutter; And this is writ to let you know, What Frank for others used to do, Is now for Frank done by another.

Here again one perceives how keen an eye these old fellows always had to a cheap adver- tisement. Writing from memory (I have a considerable collection of these things) I think it is in. the churchyard of High Ercall, .Salop, on the headstone of one Philip Roe, parish clerk, that the following summing up of the deceased's professional qualities. appears: The vocal powers here let us, mark, • Of 'Philip, our late parish clerk; - In church none. ever heard a layman With a clearer voice say " Amen!" Who now with hallelujah's sound, Like him; can make the roofs.resound The choir lament his choral tones, The town as well—here lie his bones. " Bobby" Lowe. -

There have been burlesque epitaphs, of course, and some of the most severe of these in my time were. at the expense of Robert Lowe, "that most exasperating of Victorian statesmen," as a brother scribe once termed hip. I have copies of the lot, but do not purpose to reproduce them now. It remains true, as I believe, that more jests and legends have gathered about him than about any other politician of his time, " Dizzy " only: ex- cepted. He it was, as "my older readers will recollect, who " raised Cain" by proposing to tax matches as a means of revenue raising. The immediate effect was to temporarily send up the .price of the article, panic-stricken householders hastening to buy up stocks at increased rates. Ultimately the hateful thing was killed by ridicule; it was contemptuously laughed out of Parliament, and its creator was never forgiven. I confess I had no sym- pathy with the opposition, general as it was. It has always seemed to me that in any time of crisis—say like the present—a small tax on matches would be a legitimate and reasonable way of adding to the resources of the State, and, incidentally, would minimise the waste now to be witnessed anywhere at any hour of the day.

On -Friendship.. The Rev. Hugh Williamson, whose marriage to a Warrington lady and removal to pastures new were recorded in last Saturday's issue, in his farewell sermon at Bold-street Wes- leyan Church, spoke on friendship. By-the- bye, Hartley Coleridge has some lines to a newly-married friend—but I must not let my pen run away with me; .here indeed is a sub- ject on which one might write at length. Let me just state squite briefly what has most exercised my mind of late years. That fine old writer, Sir Thomas Browne, says sweetly enough, "It is not the tears of our eyes only, but of our friends also, that do exhaust the, current of our sorrows; which, falling into many streams, runs more peaceably, and is contented with a narrow channel." In an essay on "Disagreeable people," . Hazlitt argues that there are some who, owing to shortcomings of their own, have no friends, do not make friends; worse still, out of their own conceited selfishness prefer solitude to company. These—the Browne and Hazlitt varieties—represent extremes. There be others, as I can personally testify. In my time I have edited four weekly newspapers in Lancashire and Yorkshire. 04 the last of these I spent ten years, and apparently made a host of friends. Leading public men in the district regularly dropped in to chat, • and .often said smooth and pleasant things about my work. Of course it was part of my busi- ness to exalt them and commend their doings, and some of them were so-amenable to flattery that one might "lay it .on with a trowel," as the phrase goes. However, this is the point: they professed themselves my. friends. Even- tually, an accident necessitating prolonged absence from the office and inability to write led to my supersession: Naturally, on meet, jag any of those. with. whom I had been on, such terms of intimacy I wished to explain the circumstances; but, at first to my utter bewilderment, "they all with one consent began to make excuse "—pretending that they had pressing business engagements, and so on. When at length the truth dawned on my reluctant mind, I first felt humiliated, then amused (for happily I needed not their help in , any way), and finally, I fear, some- what cynical. I imagine that, until within the last few years, I have been ,peculiarly unfortunate in my supposed friendships. At Blackpool this season I met one who had spent four years in Warrington, and had found. the people here more really and truly friendly and sympathetic than in most places. - I re- turned—in all sincerity, I do assure my ex- cellent friends—that that was precisely my --own experience.,

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 28, 1915.

Germany endeavoured to force her culture upon France, Belgitim, and Russia, and, if we had stood out, our turn would have come next, and we should have had our hands full. We must import as little of everything as we possibly could, because at• a time like the present, im- ports had to be paid for with gold, instead.of by exports, as in normal times. WASTE 'NOT, WANT NOT.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
DO THE PEOPLE UNDERSTAND?

The 'local thrift campaign has been continued 'Luring the past week, meetings having been held on Saturday, Monday and Thursday evenings. At Bridge- Foot on Saturday, Alderman Ben- nett should have presided, but, in his absence through indisposition, the gap was filled in by Mr. W. Peter Rylands (chairman of the Thrift Committee). On the platform, in addition, were Councillor Bratherton (Salford), Mr. Charles Plinston and Mr. J. Jarman.

ning. Great Britain Was that nation. It was no use Britain being a rich nation unless her wealth was organised so as to bring the war to as quick an end as possible. It could be done by thrift; it could be done by every person who was in a position on Monday morning to do so, putting five shillings in the Post Office Savings Bank. They would be given a voucher which would immediately begin to bear interest at four and a half per cent.. And when the amount had risen from five to twenty shillings, the individual who was saving went again to the Post Office and obtained a fresh script. When the total had reached £5, the owner of the account became a shareholder in the British Empire—the greatest concern the world had ever seen. (Applause.) Let them look at . the generosity of the interest. The Government was giving four and a half per cent. where in- vestors had previously been content with two and a half. And this fine interest was given by a Government that had behind it the full resources of our Empire! Was dyer such in- terest given with such security?

If You Want To Sell. After the war a slump would set in. Busi- ness would decline, and at that moment men would be running after stock that carried four and a half per cent. interest, or that had be- hind it the full ,resources of the British Empire. So no one need be afraid to invest their money in the way he had suggested., It had been said that the money invested .cold not be touched for a number of years. Let them be assured that if they ever wanted to

===== 0002601_19150828_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT ASKED TO HALF STARVE.

The Chairman, remarked that the subject with which they had to deal that night was a dull one: the duty of the British citizens in the matter of war-time economy- Nevertheless, it was extremely important. We could not all fight at the Front, but those who stayed be- hind could do something. Alen, munitions and money were the three great factors (in the war. They were not that night concerned with men nor with munitions, although he hoped there was nobody in Warrington who did not appreciate the need of both, and who was not prepared to help to provide one or the other of them. It was with therthird factor they had to deal that evening: money. Every shilling we wasted was a shillin less available for our country to carry on the war. And every man who could. make a shilling go a little further than before the war began was doing something to help. It was not the wish of the Parliamentary War Savings Committee that the movement should take the form of parsimony in regard 'to the necessities of life. The country woald-be no better f off by half starving itself, and the first ;thing to see to Was that our .families and dependents were well provided with the necessi- ties of life. _ _ _

The Enjoyment Of Money.

There must be those who, after providing necessities, had nothing left over. Nothing could be asked of people so placed. But there were a great many in this country, rich and poor, who had opportunities of spending their money a little mom wisely than perhaps they would in normal times. When we had money. we were rather inclined to enjoy wasting it. Many people had said that the real enjoyment derived from money was not got out of that portion spent on necessities, but out of that spent upon things not really needed, but desired. In normal times, he, personally, never begrudged anyone "seeking enjoyment, but the present times were not normal, and it behoved us all, in whatever walk of life, to see to it that we did not waste, and that wherever pos- sible we saved by a little more economy in household expenditure and management, and in all ways. The object of that movement, which was being taken up throughout the length and breadth of the land, was to get people to accumulate in the Post Office and other banks savings of which many of them would probably be subsequently in need, owing to trade depres- sion. Beanos were a very good thing in times of peace, but in times of war the money normally spent in that form of enjoyment would be better saved. Every morning we should ask ourselves what we could do to help the country to support those brave men who were fighting for usNacross the seas. (Applause.) BRITAIN'S BEAUTIFUL CHARACTER- ISTICS.

Councillor' Bratherton said that during the past eight months we had imported £100,000,000 more worth of goods than we did last year. That was a pleasing fact to be able to record, although we were supposed to be blockaded by the Kaiser. (Laughter.) It was a dummy blockade, and was, therefore, .not of much value to Germany... (Hear; hear, and, applause.) During the mai() time we, 'had exported A:100,000,000 less, but that was accounted for by the fact thabt 3,000,000 to 4,000,000 men had been withdrawn from civil life to take ,up arms. The country was expending ..2,3 000,000 Per 'day, or .22,083 every minute. That money had to be paid sometime, because Great Britain never repudiated "scraps of paper." That was one of her beautiful characteristics. She did not act as Germany acted in matters of this kind. The money Great Britain was expending she was not receiving, and when the war ended every penny required would have to be drawn out of the bone, sinew and marrow of the people, and because that had to be done there was all the greater need for each one of them, men and women, to make the most elaborate preparations for the rainy day which was sure to come. (Hear, hear.) Business More Than Usual.

Why. would, it come? The wa'r would some day end, and When it ended the necessity for the production of munitions of war would cease, and a million of men be thrown out of employ- ment for the time being, and would want work of another kind. Then, three out of the four millions of men who were to-day bearing arms would come back to civil life. And because of the disorganisation that would come to trade, because of the upsetting of business in every part of the county, the slump would set in, and the rainy day would have begun. What ought we to do? We were prosperous just at present. Business was not as usual; it was a little bit more than as usual. It was not in the same channel. It could not very well be in the same channel when three milliofl men had been drawn from civil life to serve in the Army, and another million drawn from the peaceful pursuits of coiamerce to make instruments and materials for the killing of men. Business was not as usual. In fact, it was probably not too much to say that there never was a time within the period of the oldest person in that audience when it was so easy to get it. job and when wages ruled so high as to-day. Therefore, he suggested to them that, in their own interests, as well as in the interests of the country, they should begin to practise the principles of thrift.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
How Germany Economises.

lle was not going to suggest that they should eat less bread. That would be cruel. Bread was the principal staff of life, but he wanted them to remember that, whilst last year we imported into this country £25,000,000 worth of flour in the first seven months, this year, up to date, we had imported £45,000,000 worth. Whilst they should not eat less he suggested that they should all at once begin to practise economy in regard to the portion that had hitherto been wasted in every home. When the rulers of Germany wanted to husband their food supplies, the first thing they did was to collar the entire supplies of the empire and then issue to every family or individual a ticket 'entitling them to so much food per week, and it was not a very generous allowanpe, either. And no person in Germany, no matter how much money they might possess, could get more than the allotted quantity. Much as we despised German methods and practises, there was one thing at least which we might. with advantage to our- selves, learn from Germany, and that was their method of conserving their food supplies. Every person in this country who to-day practised economy and thrift was doing some- thing to help to bring to a close the terrible war we all so much deplored. Cut Down Your Beer!

Turning from the subject of food, the speaker proceeded to deal with the drink phase of the question.. He was not a teetotaler, but he sug- gested that we were drinking: far too much in this country. -*The matter of drinking was largely:a question for each to decide for him- self or herself. There was a very wide range of quantities. One man was brought up in a Police Court recently on a charge of being drunk, and he denied that he was ,drunk. An inquiry by the, magistrates as to how much beer he consumed on the day on which he was arrested, elicited the reply from the prisoner that he only had sixteen pints. Well, he (the speaker) suggested that the man who drank sixteen pints should cut his allowance down to eight; that the Man who had eight should keep himself to four, and that the man' who was in the habit of taking four should confine himself to two. They were not out to advocate the cutting down of the articles of daily consump- tion below the point of necessity, but above that point it was the duty of every man and woman to economise in the present terrible time. The Nation With The Longest Purse.

The war was a war of exhaustion, in which the nation possessing the longest purse was going to have the best possible chance. of win-

realise on their investments because a rainy day had come along, they had only to go to the Post Office and ask to have their Stock sold. Within three or four days their stock would be sold, and their full expenditure would be realised, minus ninepence. This applied to any sum up to t 25. The' ninepence that would be deducted would be a com- mission. "Go away," Councillor Brather- ton concluded, "and on Monday Morn- ing become partners in the British Empire, so doing something to show your determination to help your country to bring to a successful issue the damnable war in which we are to-day engaged." (Applause)

===== 0002601_19150828_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
The Difference.

No -doubt many of .them had heard of the <difference between German government and British government. When the German Emperor came to the throne he gave his pro- clamation to the Army and three days later to the people, and the proclamation was to the effect that the people existed for the Army. That was a taste of German culture, and we in this country didn't want the same thing intro- duced here. We wanted to be in a position at the close of the War to ensure that no further danger should exist of Germany's being able to bring her " culture " either here or to any other of the European countries. They could leave Germany to themselves, because the

===== 0002601_19150828_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
Relief Of Tired Munition Workers.

lie wanted the people to say *to themselves: Can I employ My time in work that will be of more benefit to the country and be getting better money? Many people asking themselves such a question would be compelled to answer in the affirmative, and should lose no time in act- ing accordingly. It would be a fine thing if an organised effort were made to put on foot a: scheme for relieving tired munition workers on Sundays' and Saturday afternoons by engaging the services for those days of people who during the week worked elsewhere. They would then earn additional money, which they could save. In that way they would be help- ing the Government in two ways, because the munition workers who had had the week-end rest would return to work feeling better able to put their full energy into the work that was so important. It might be said that he was advising the breaking of the Sabbath, but our soldiers and sailors were fighting on Sundays and dying on Sundays, and if it was all right for them to fight and die on Sunday, who should, say it was not all right to work for the same cause on Sunday? The Government And Coming Slump. •

No doubt Britain could easily obtain a loan of <2600,000,000 from America, but that would entail the payment of £27,000,000 annually as interest, whereas by borrowing the money from the people of the country the Government kept the whole of the money at home. It was con- servinc, the nation's wealth, and, at the same time, con- serving the people. By exercising thrift and economy, they would be providing more, shots for the nation's locker. There was One thing that would have to be httended to .after the war, and that was the matter of the returned soldiers all of whom should be offered their jobs back if. they wanted to take them. The Government would have to deal firmly with the industrial situation at the close .of the war. If it was necessary to take hold of the industries for the fighting of the war, it was also necessary to take hold of them and see that there was no, slump after- wards. In conclusion, Mr. Maginn said Ile felt sure that all of his listeners, would be *Min.. to make almost any sacrifice if they felt it ° was necessary to prevent the success of the German arms, and he appealed to each and all carefully to consider things and to do their best. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150828_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
ECONOMISE AND STOP THE WAR.

Miss Broadbent made a brief but spirited appeal to the audience to practise economy in all directions, so that the horrible war that was devouring the husbands and sons and fathers of the country should cease at the earliest possible moment.

IRONY OF TIIE PRESENT SITUA-

===== 0002601_19150828_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
TION.

The final meeting of the local thrift cam- paign was held at the foot of 'Lovely-lane on Thursday evening, when Mr. W. H. Sherburn presided, supported by Miss Broadbent, Mrs. Hall, Mr. Joseph Elwell and Mr. Felix Maginn.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
LEARNING THE. LESSONS.

The Chairman said that :at a time like the present, when the civilisation of Europe was wasting its resources in human destruction, it seemed very much like irony to tell people who never had much money in their lives to begin to be more thrifty than usual. The war had taught us at least one thing: how greatly we depended on each other. It should be the aim of all of us to try to get the best out of the awful war that was now raging—to learn the lessons it taught. We needed to clear our vision and make our minds pure, so that we could think the les- sons out.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
Good Resolutions and I-km to Keep Them.

If there was one way more than another in which he would ask his hearers to look at the thrift question it was this.: Some of them were having better times, so far as the financial aspect was concerned,- than they had had for a long time, and who could blame them if, out of their surplus income they should go in for a little extra pleasure? It was quite natural that they should feel that desire.. But let them make sure that their recreation was of- the right kind —that it was such pleasure as did not leave a sting behind it. It was waste that was being deoried, not legitimate expenditure,' and ,at a time like the present money could be saved. Some people, when they decided to save money, kept their savings in the house, where it was accessible at any time when they felt inclined to put their fingers in and spend on something that was not necessary; and by the end of the week, perhaps," the good resolution. and the money as well had vanished. The best plan was when a good resolution to save was made, to put the motley in the Post Office Savings Bank whilst the resolution was hot.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
THE THREE LINES OF DEFENCE.

Mr. Felix Maginn observed that the question for every Briton to ask himself or herself just at present was: What can I do to help my' country in this hour of supreme trial? Everyone could not fight. If every workman threw down his tools and rushed to the Front, the Army result- ing would be a magnificent one, but it would not long be in existence. The Army was our first line of defence; the munition workers:con- Stituted the second line; and the third and largest line was that made up of thoge quiet, unobtrusive citizens who, day after day, west on their way and kept their own counsel. They served the country in an important degree by spending their money advisedly and cutting down Unessential expenses; by keeping them- Selves in good health and spirits, suppressing all baseless excitement, and going about their daily duties in the ordinary way, doggedly and per- sistently holding out against had news and re- fusing to believe it possible that we could be beaten. In Conclusion, the speaker said it was, a fine thing that the great trade unions had invested their money in the War Loan. It was a good example to the people.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WORKING MAN'S DINNER.

Miss Broadbent, in appealing to the house- wife to be economical in her management, said she didn't think people altogether realised that it was money; more than anything else, that would decide whether England or Germany won the war. She was fully aware that the average working man's wife did not need the advice she was giving, but there were many women who, after sitting on the doorstep most ,of the morn- ing, rushed indoors at the last moment and flung a piece of meat into the frying pan, or sent out for some fish and chips. (Laughter.) That was not economy. She came across a case the other day of a woman who provided for her hus- band's dinner an Eccles cake and some tea. (Laughter.) Miss Broadbent went on to advo- cate fewer visits to the cinema and the consump- tion of less beer, among other items, and con- cluded by urging everyone to be as thrifty and economical as possible, for the sake, if for no other • reason, of "our splendid fellows at the Front."

HELP YOURSELVES!

Mr. Elwell said that the cry was: Help the Government! He said to them: Help your- selves! The money the Government was spend- ing on the war was money obtained from the people. We didn't want our brothers, fathers and sons at .the Front to go short of anything necessary to help to bring the war to a speedy issue, and to economise was to help the gallant men in the trenches and on the high seas.

A RED RAG TO A BULL.

Mr. Jarman said that two many working men Mr. Smith said that too laity working men had so little in the way of wages, particularly before the war, that it was like putting a rEd rag before a bull to talk to them about thrift It was not expected of. those whose work was cut out to make both ends meet, that they should- save, but of those who could do so it was expected now as a national duty. Many people, witlipossibly some reasop, said "What has posterity done. for me thtit I should trouble?" But this was not a time to talk like that. The nation was fighting for its life, and it was up to all of them to do their little bit to save the situation. As surely as the war would end some time, so surelY Would the money saved from now be needed to meet the temporary slump, due to many causes, one of which mould be the return of an enormous number of men from. the Front, making it necessary for the complete reorganisation . of industry in the country.

Mr. J. Elwell presided. at Monday night's meeting at the foot of Bewsey-street. He was supported by Mrs. Hall, Miss Broadbent, and Mr. Felix Maginn. The Chairmait outlined the object Of the meeting. Why were they appealing for thrift?, For three reasons: firstly, in order to help in

COMPLETE FAITH IN BRITISH GENIUS.

Mr. Felix Maginn started with the cheerful remark that he felt that this country was cap- able of accomplishing whatsoever she put her hand to. He had a complete faith in the genius of the British people. He believed it was only necessary for the people to under- stand the subject thoroughly, and success would follow. And if there was any falling away from the high standard of success in the, present war he held the opinion that it was because the people had not properly realised the magnitude of the task that had 'alien upon us. Reading the papers, many of them felt, he for one, that the previous week was the blackest week the British Empire had experienced since the black week in the South African war, but he was glad to see that the news was better, °and he felt that things had taken a turn. He did not wish to pose as an alarmist, but he thought the country was in danger. It was in danger from foes without, but it was his opinion that the *chief danger consisted in the fact that the people did not fully realise the magnitude of the issue at stake. And it was not alto- gether their fault.

Dangerous Optimism.

Britain had been so uniformly successful dur- ing the last two or three hundred years that the people could not conceive the possibility of our losing. This was perhaps only natural, but there was danger in it. Britain had won in the Past because she was determined, resolute and sacrificing, and she would do go this time by a display of the same qualities. The papers were partly to -blame for the undue optimism of the people. They told us last year that the war would be over by Christmas. • But, though it was 'a much bigger. task than had been gener- ally.anticipated, it was not beyond our capacity if we tackled it in the spirit we had tackled big Problems before. It had been said that that campaign had been organised to get money out of the pockets of • the working people. That was not true. It was organised to persuade everybody, both for their own good and that of the country, to be economical and thrifty. He

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

Women Workers And The Loads They Can Carry.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BLACKWATER

DISTRICT.

(Queries and correspondence should be addressed to "The Hub," c/o The Editor. Replies can- not be sent by post and all queries will be answered in our columns.)

The Blackwater is one Of the most lovely of all Irish rivers, although it is comparatively little known. The usual Irish tourist centres seem to monopolise all attention, but many of the spots on the Blackwater are far lovelier than even Killarney itself. Make Fermoy—a large mili- tary centre—the starting point. Fermoy is on the main line from Waterford to Cork, and the principal trains, including the Irish mail from Rosslare to Cork, and vice-versa, stop there. From Fermoy the Cappoquin road is taken, pass- ing on the way Ballyduff and Lismore Bridge. The most famous spot upon the river is Cappo- quill, where the views are superb. At Lismore, a quarter of a mile away, the Duke of Devon- shire has a castle, which can be seen for miles around. There is excellent fishing in the Black- water, especially in the neighbourhood of Cap- poquin. At Cappoquin the southern road is taken and from thence to Youghal one rides'along the east side of the river all the way. Some of the views are very fine indeed, and the road is an easy one. En route are passed Bewley cross roads, Villierstown and Clashmore. Youghal, an ancient and pretty town, is partly a holiday resort and partly a port with a small coasting and fishing trade. Sir Walter Raleigh was chief magistrate here ,in 1588, and his house is still standing, in the garden of which he planted the first potato in Ireland. At Youghal the train can be taken for Cork, or else one can return to Fermoy,' the starting point by road. In the latter case, from Youghal proceed to Mount Uniache, a charming spot; thence via Mona- varnage, Ballynoe and Campbell's cross roads. The mileage of this trip is 661, and presents a remarkable variety of scenery from the charm- ing valley of the Blackwater to the rugged and barren moors between Fermoy and Youghal, and it.should always be included in any journey to the South of Ireland.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VARIABLE GEAR AND ITS CARE.

It is not very long ago since a three-speed gear was considered something in the nature, of a luxury. To-day it is lo.oked upon as much a necessity as the free wheel, i.e., if the full enjoy- ment of, cycling is to be obtained. A necessity it may be, but few owners appear to realise that the variable gear is a very delicate piece of workmanship, and as suCh requires careful treatment and proper lubrication. In reality, the three-speed gear is very similar to the gear of a motor car, but on a smaller scale. Every cyclist should see a three-speed hub when taken to pieces, and then it would be understood that changing gear is an operation that should be done with great care., It is always best to free wheel when changing either up or down, but if this is' not possible. then pedal slowly. Lubri- cating is important, and this must not be for- gotten.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
HEAVY LOADS ON CYCLES.

Everyone must have noticed the very heavy load that tradesmen's boys are called upon to propel either on cycles or carrier tricycles. It seems as though this abuse had entered into other departments of life, and this is indicated by the following, published in a cycling contem- porary :—" It is stated in the Press that Miss Philippa Fawcett, daughter of a former Postmaster-General, is taking over a postman's round in a rural part of Suffolk, and she has been informed that as the ,varcels she will have to carry may in many cases be too heavy for a woman's machine she will have to,use the Post Office bicycle, which will necessitate the wearing of gymnasium costume.' The Obvious reply to this statement is that parcels which are' too heavy for a woman's machine are too heavy for a woman.' It is simply absurd to suggest that a load sufficiently heavy to cause O. lady's bicycle of the current type to collaje or to place it in danger, is a suitable load for an average woman to carry round vital Suffolk, or anywhere else. BY all means let us eidist the services of women for this class of w,Jrk, but let us exercise reasonable judgment in allotting them appropriate tasks."

===== 0002601_19150828_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
SIDECAR ALIGNMENT.

The statement is often made that a sidecar combination is uhmechanical. This may be true in a sense, but practical experienct demon- strates that some outfits have been run for years and are still sound. There is bound to be a considerable strain on the motor cycle, but this depends in a great measure on the alignment. As a general rule one sees the motor cycle lean- ing towards the sidecar, and it is said that this

relieves the machine of a good part of the strain. I saw recently that a suggestion haa been put forward by a writer in a technical paper, to the effect that the most satisfactory results are obtained when the atachment is se. made that the motor cycle leans away from the. sidecar. On the surface there appears to be a, lot, of sense in this suggestion, but before I say any more on the subject I am going to test it myself.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
SHORTAGE IN PETROL TINS.

Mueh has been written with reference to the short measure given by some petrol dealers. have on a previous occasion referred to the matter in these columns. Hence, the follow- ing, which recently appeared in a motoring' contemporary, is particularly interesting:— " Recently in the course of our -travels ill various parts of the country we have noticed two factors which appear to be not unrelated. In the first place the quality of the ordinary two-gallon petrol tin does not appear to be so good as in pre-war times, and in the second case we have noticed that the tins did' net always appear to hold the full two-gallon mea- sure. Now, it may be well that ° these two points are of a correlative character. In fact, lye would go further and say that it appears more than likely that there is, a relationship between the two. If other motorists have noticed a shortage in the spirit supplied in tins of petrol we would suggest that they should not be toe hasty in condemning the petrol companies. It would seem that the following train of thought is likely enough an accurate diagnosis of the state of affairs. Enormous numbers of . the newer and best quality tins may have been re- quisitioned for use 'somewhere in France,' and elsewhere, and the corresponding deficit in the ordinary trade circles of such cans .cannot be made good immediately. Consequently the ,petrol purveyors—on the assumption that this. premise is accurate—would have to draw upon their supplies of old.tins which were en route to the Scrap heap.' It is, of course, well known that old tins are, inclined- to be leaky." • .Perhaps i- is the real solution. but I "hae ma doots."

WHITE HEATHER DAY.

Vaivable Assistance For Worthy Warrington Objects.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
CRICKET GROUND FETE.

The deplorable effects of this cruel war, which is decimating so many of our gallant regi- ments in the fighting-line, call for our sym- pathetic and practical help for these brave fellows who emerge from the trenches. Our local Infirmary and similar organisations deserve every support which can be given them for the valuable and noble services rendered to the sufferers who come to Warrington for medical care and attention. Whilst we seek to relieve the sufferings of those brought home to us and to lighten the burden some will carry with them all their days, let us not for- get the heroes who have fallen in the battle for truth and right—" the flowers of the forest that are a' wede away."

In -this letter, sent in his unfortunate absence, Mr. J. Fulton-Smith summed up the sentiments of, the Caledonian Association in respect to their annual White Heather Day. This year it was held in aid of three of the most deserving Warrington philanthropies—the In- firmary, the Nursing Association and the Poor Children's Summer Camp. Naturally the assistance required by these institutions in times of war i§ of greater urgency than in normal times; and therefore the promoters of the festival extended their scale proportionately. With the co-operation of the Works' Bowling Association they folhAved the popular fashion. of token-selling on the streets, and also held in the afternoon a fete in the Arpley Cricket Field (kindly lent by the ,Cricket Bowling and Tennis, Club). Through- Out the day the young ladies in the main parts of the town continued to sell their white heather sprigs, and few pedestrians were to be seen without one.

FATAL COLLISION.

Motovist Killed Near The Dog

And Dart Inn.

There was a tragic accident near the Dog and Dart Inn, Grappenhall, on, Sunday morn- ing, involving the death of Mr. Joseph James (46), of 15, Haydock-street, Earlestown, who was a foreman cabinet maker for Messrs. G. and J. Weaver, .Of Bridge-street, Warrington. About_ half-past eleven on Sunday mprning Mr. James was driving a motor . cycle, with side-car. attached, along the Knutsford-road, Grappenhall. He was accompanied by his wife, who was in the.side-car, and his daughter, who was riding on the carrier behind. They turned into the Lymm-road by the Dog and Dart Inn, and just then they noticed two motor cyclists travelling in a direction opposite to their own. One was ridden by a man named It-dstage. of Altrincham, and the other by a man named Oldham, a butcher, of Broadheath, Altrincham. There was a collision between Mr. James' cycle-car and the leading cycle, and both over- turned. Mr. James was thrown over the handle-bars of his machine, close to the pave- ment on the near side of the road. He was rendered unconscious. No one else was seriously hurt. Mr. James was taken to the Warrington, In- firmary with all speed, and it was discovered that he was suffering from a fracture of the skull. He died at twenty minutes past nine in the evening.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
INQUEST OPENED.

An inquest was convened by Coroner Brig- house at the Warrington Coroner's Court on Wednesday evening, but the Coroner announced that as Mr. Rust:ge was ill in bed he would merely take evidence of identification.. Mrs. Alice James, wife of deceased, identified the body, and stated that she was present at 9.20 on Sunday night when her husband passed away.

The Coroner informed Mrs. James that he would give her a certificate to bury the body when she thought fit. In answer to Mr. Thomas Cooper, who ap- peared for the widow, the Coroner said he did not think it was wise to say anything about the cause of the accident, except that deceased was knocked off and became unconscious.

The inquest was adjourned' till September-Bth at 10.15 a.m.

MARGARINE FOR BUTTER.

Local Guardians Commencing

To Economise.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held on Wednesday morning, Mr. A. J. Jolley presiding. The Cottage Homes Committee's minutes con- tained a recommendation that margarine be sub- stituted ,for butter. Mr. Shaw said that the minute, as the Clerk had read it, was a misrepresentation of the committee's recommendation. What they sug- gested was that margarine should be substituted for a portion of the butter. Mrs. : That's what I, understood.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell said the ;committee tliought that the Guardians ought to do some- thing to carry out the suggestions, indeed the demands, of the Local Government Board circu- lar with regard to economy. The best margar- ine was equal to butter. The 'suggestion was not to substitute an inferior article, but to substi- tute one.good thing, for another. The Medical Officer, Mr. Shaw 'added, was present when the matter was discussed, and recommended that margarine be substituted for a portion of the butter used. He said that it was quite as good as butter. The recommendation was approved, and the minutes adopted.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Windsor Of Russia.

The Windsor of Russia, where the Czar has a wonderful palace, is situated about fifteen miles from Petograd. In times of peace it is a gay little town, but the war has transformed it into a big hospital. The Czaritza and her elder daughters decided to go through a month's training at a leading hospital, and at the end of that period they passed the usual examina- tions most successfully. The beautiful palace at Tsarskoe .Selo is the principal hospital in the little town, and over it the Empress and. her daughters preside, working as ordinary 11111 quietly, but most eflici:mtly. Every eay trainloads of the wounied arrive at the Czar's private station, and are tra nspol:el to the palace. The devotion of the simple Russian peasant soldiers to ' the Empress and her daughters is most touching, and their gratitude is unbounded.

Women Radiographers. The Council of the . Royal Astronomical Society has recently added a clause to its charter, which was necessary 'in order to admit women as members. , A few years ago the Society wished to honour 'Madame- Curie with the Davy medal, but its stringent rules forbade even the presence of the lady within its walls. This disability is now happily removed. Women doctors have proved singularly expert in the new science of radiography, inaugurated by Madame Curie, and the London Society for Women's Suffrage has lately provided a splendidly equipped '-ray motor ambulance for the Women's War Hospital at Rayaumont in France. Dr. •Agnes Savill,- the expert radio- grapher of that hospital, and Dr. Frances Ivens, will use the car, -which will be staffed entirely by women.

Irishwomen. And. The War. The war 'activities of Irish women have been both comprehensive- and practical. After the declaration of war, an Emergency Council was formed in Dublin, with branches all over Ire- land, to deaf with the unemployment of women.

HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

An. Economical Soup. This is the time to make and serve the French vegetable bouillon—so wholesome, so relatively inexpensive, and so tasty. It is also very easy to make. Soak overnight about half a pound of good white haricot beans. In the morning put them into a saucepan or stewpan holding about five quarts of water. Add then a certain quantity of all the fresh vegetables available— carrots, turnips, onions, leeks, a lettuce, a little thyme and parsley, and anything else you like. Let simmer for five hours, until the five quarts of water are reduced to two. The soup may then be eaten as it is, or made more delicate by being passed through the sieve, and, if necessary, slightly thickened with 'tapioca. A spoonful of cream may also be stirred in.— "Manchester Guardian."

The Care Of The Hands. Every man and woman admires a beautifully shaped hand, but,. unfortunately, everyone has not got this much envied possession. The possessor of square or spade-shaped fingers, how- ever, may take this .comfort—such fingers usually show resolution and ability to overcome obstacles rather than give in to them. To know that one's hands say this should be a great encouragement, and much can be done, especially during the summer, to make and keep the hands in good condition. White hands are always much admired, but to me they seem 'a sign of ill-health or indolence, as no man or woman can have "lily-white" hands if he or she is doing a share of the world's work. The woman who must do her own housework should make it a rule to have two or three pairs of loose-fitting gloves ready to slip on when required. If you clean a grate, sift dust, sweep a room. or do any other work which makes the hands dirty. it means the immediate washing of them and the temptation not to take enough time to . dry properly. resulting in constantly chapped hands and the gradual losing of any prettiness.—Maud, in "Farm and Home."

Barley Water. Barley water is useful as a remedy .for colds and catarrhs, and, when taken for coughs and chest ttoubles, 4. is usually flavoured with liquorice, figs, and raisins: To make it proceed as follows: Take four tablespoonfuls or 2oz. of either pot or flaked barley, wash same, and put into one or two pints of clean, cold water, pre- ferably distilled. Bring to the boil and simmer for from five to twenty minutes. The time allowed should be regulated according to the consistence required. The more glutinous, the longer should it boil. When done to the re- quired consistence the water should be strained and served up very hot. To season, just before time has expired for boiling add strips of lemon rind and some lemon juice. Gum arabic can be added to increase demulcency, and honey or

A Wet Weather Hat.

• The abnormally wet weather of July and August, with the destructive thunderstorms and floods has bibught into prominence the improvements recently made in the method ,of waterproofing materials of all kinds. Not only are all fabrics .used for outer • garments, capable of being rendered impervious to the heaviest downloour, but such fragile goods as tulle, chiffon and lace can be subjected to the same process without detriment, and with equal success. . Even milliners are using waterproof feathers and flowers. For a smart wet weather hat, to be worn when an umbrella cannot be carried, a sailor shape in patent leather cloth cannot be beaten. The cloth should be moulded to the crown, and the trimming be of the same material, a waterproofed quill or two complet- ing the whole. Or if preferred, the hat may have a wreath of uncurled ostrich feathers, which merely requires shaking out after the heaviest rain to rcstore its pristine beauty. Wings and breasts of game birds, if carefully cured, are naturally weather proof, and they are always- used by high-class makers of sports hats.

Large and exceedingly handsome jet fastenings are employed for even- ing wraps, made of materials tAat sparkle and scintillate with spangles and cut beads, for spangles have come back again, and are seen in black, white and various colours. Ruches are somewhat lavishly used on the hems of skirts, more especially of lace dresses. Fringe has also

Any cold steamed fish, an equal quantity or boiled potatoes, one egg, a tablespoonful of chopped parsley, pepper and salt, a little flour, and breadcrumbs. Rob the potatoes And fish through a sieve, put them in a saucepan with the chopped parsley, pepper, salt, a little butter and milk, and stir until it is a hot creamy mass. Take it off the fire and stir in half a well-beaten egg. Turn the mixture on to a dish, and when cool enough to handle form it into cakes or cork shapes. Flour them, then dip in egg and bread- crumbs, and fry till a golden brown.—" Girl's Realm."

Whose Will? I -777ft7+77

"My husband,",' remarked a Philadelphia matron to a group of friends, "was a confirmed smoker with a tobacco heart when I married him a year ago, but to-day he never touches the weed."

"Good," said one of the group. "To break off a lifetime habit requires a strong will." "Well, that's what I've got," said the wife. —"Exchange."

Marriages Among Heathens. The more one studies the problem of marriages the more plain does it become, declares a writer in the "London Chronicle," that many of the heathen ideas on the subject are infinitely. superior to ours. One of the dreams of Socialist reformers, for instance, is the endowment of motherhood. They regard it as a Utopian vision of the far future not likely to be fulfilled for years to come.

Among the Mohammedans this dream is a reality. The maintenance of children devolves so exclusively on the father that the mother is entitled to claim wages for nursing them. 'lite importance of her services to the State in rearing healthy citizens is thus recognised in the most practical manner, We hear a good deal of agitation nowadays about making the couditions of divorce equal to both sexes. Among the Shawanese this is already done. An unfaithful husband can be turned adrift by his wife, who retains all his property. They go one better and make drunk- enness also an. offence for which divorce can be obtained.

"42, Wakefield-street, Warrington, August 16th, 1915. , "Dear Uncle Ned.—A holiday in the coun- try is very pleasant. You can romp in the fields and enjoy yourselves, and there are plenty of nice walks, too. It is also very

made its appearance, the flounces of some of th* ' smartest taffetas gowns are edged with a silk fringe about an inch in depth. Buttonhole stitch is at the present moment tiling the place of picot edging On blouses and muslin collars. Coarse, rough cotton or silk is used, and the work• is quickly done. A white serge suit had very shalloW.scallops at the edge of the coat, and at the hem of the skirt, and button- hole stitch was effectively used for both. Silk braid makes a neat trimming for simple tailor mades, in serge or suitings.

Shoes, Boots, and Stockings. Hosiery and footgear will be of iirime im- portance this autumn ,and winter, since short skirts are to prevail. Stockings must of necessity be dearer, because the cheap German and Austrian makes are not to be had. Foot gear, too, will rise in price as the winter conies on. At the moment shoes and boots of croco- dile leather are among the most attractive in appearance, and reasonable in price. They are not cheap, but they wear remarkably well, and have a distinction all their own. In a rich dark brown, this leather takes a high polish, and makQs strong Oxford shoes for country wear. The tops of high boots are frequently made of .materials to match the suits with which they are worn. Antelope, a particularly soft leather, makes handsome boots that pro- vide protection and support for weak ankles. Kid of many kinds, velvet, and cloth are all employed for uppers by the manufacturer, whose chief aim is to use as little leather as possible.

War Jewellery. In the, campaign against luxury. no special Mention is Made of the self-denying ordinance against wearing, jewellery. There can, how- ever, be no doubt that the war has hit jewel- lers very badly, but they are doing a good trade in engagement and wedding rings. New designs in lockets and frames for photographs are also in great demand, and war jewellery in the shape of badges, brooches, &c., is im- mensely popular. The National Register has given an extra fillip to war weddings, and there is also an unprecedented demand for en-, gagement rings; In the ordinary way very little jewellery is being worn, and there is a tendency to return to old-fashioned ornaments, such as heavy gold chains, antique brooches, and necklaces.

HINTh ON HEALTH.

Useful Information In Popular

Why You Sneeze.

There is more than one cause for sneezing, and persons may differ in their susceptibility. A bright light will cause some persons to sneeze:: the pollen of certain plants will affect others, and most people are likely to sneeze in the pre- sence of dust. Such ,sneezing is due to super- ficial irritation. The sneeze caused by the effect of cold is different. It is an attempt of Nature to cure you. She makes you sneeze for the same reason that she makes you shiver—to generate heat for warming:the blood and preventing you from taking more cold,—to help to relieve the cold you have. The sneezing from cold is an act not of the nose alone, but of the entire body, during which every muscle gives a jump. The body is affected by a spasmodic effort to warm the entire 'system and throw off the cold.

A Secret Of Good Digestion. Digestion of food begins in the mouth, and to accomplish perfectly this part of digestion the food must be thoroughly chewed, and in that process thoroughly insalivated. This will depend upon the condition of the tools you work with. Food cannot be thoroughly chewed un- less the teeth are in good condition, and every tooth lost or diseased diminishes the ability of that person to masticate his food to the extent of that lost chewing surface. You might as well expect a carpenter to turn out good job with few and poor tools as to expect your stomach to digest satisfactorily a lot of food which by reason of poor teeth, or few of them, was not sufficiently chewed. The loss of a tooth is therefore a misfortune, as just to that extent the preparatory process of digestion is crippled. If digesion is not perfect, and if assimilation is not perfect, health is impossible. —Sandford Bennett.

The Best. Temperance Drink. All of the so-called temperance drinks contain a varying percentage of alcohol and are other- wise more or less harmful. Ginger ale and beer, for example, exert a distinctly irritating effect upon the delicate lining raembiane of the stomach which ultimately may lead to gastric catarrh or some other form of stomach disorder. There is only one ideal temperance drink, and that is "Adam's Ale," fresh from the tap or the spring. The arguments against temperance drinks hold equally for alcoholic beverages. One and all, they are, strictly speaking, entirely un- necessary, and, more than that, they are unde- sirable. Alcoholic drinks are worse than the temperance drinks in that they contains much larger percentage of the poison alcohol. The intoxicating effects of alcohol upon health and vitality are always detrimental. Spirits, wines, and beers are all poisonous drinks and they serve equally to waste money and to waste health.—" Good Health."

Several new industries were set on foot, and it is hoped some may be established on a perma- nent footing. Toy Making is one of these, another is the coloured hand-embroidery —hitherto a monopoly of the Germans and Austrians. The domestic arts and sciences have not been neglected, and many young girls attend the various centres, where practical instruction is given in the cooking of food by the means and utensils to which they are accustomed. Special attention is bestowed also on the mending and making of clothes. Other activities include the management of clubs, hostels for Belgian refugees, baby clinics, schools for mothers, and cottage gardening.

CHESHIRE VOLUNTEERS.

General Creagh's Report On Chester inspection.

General Sir o.lloore Creagh, V.C., G.C.5.1., has reported as under on his inspec- tion of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment. at Chester on Bank Holiday : "The regiment is well commanded, well officered and organised, and from what I saw I concluded that all ranks have worked hardy and are devoted to their patriotic duty, and resolved to do all in their power to be in a position to defend their hearths and homes, and to be duly qualified for any particular duty. that may be assigned to them when they are told off, as they certainly will be, to theii. place in the general system of the defence of our country. "Considering the short time they have been embodied, their progress has been most satis- factory, and gives ample evidence of the hard' work done by all ranks and the esprit de corps. existing in the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment: The march-past was excellent and the turn-out and physique of the men very good.

"The -.Mounted section turned out most creditably. The men carried carbines and were very nearly all in uniform. The bitting was good and the saddlery well Etted and in good order. The men sat on their. horses well; then' turnout showed they were gooil horsemen and' horsemasters ably commanded.

"Most excellent was the work done by the motor section. It considerably helped the con-, centration' of the regiment; in fact it demon- strated the great importance of such a section in case of raid or invasion, while in ordinary work it has covered 10,152 miles in doing -Gbv- ernment duty. "The staff work done to secure this concen- tration from all directions of a number of recently-enrolled men, without confusion of any kind and with precision, is worthy of all praiSe and reflects the greatest credit on the regi- mental staff."

THE EXAMINER,

.SA.TURDAY. AUGUST 21, 1915.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANS' VAULTING AMBITION.

The run of success that has, for a few weeks, attended German arms has encouraged the enemy, as a drunken man is said to do, to speak its real mind. There has been a good deal of straining on the part of our adversaries to prove to the world that we were the aggressors, and that they were the inno- cent sufferers by reason of our wanton attack upon them L Somehow the world has not been unduly impressed by the supposed villainy of a nation with 200,000 soldiers attempting the task of uprooting a nation with 8,000,000 armed men, some of whom, before we chal- lenged them, were already on Belgian soil. tven to-day, more than twelve months after the outbreak of hostilities, we are striving to get on something like terms of equality, and though wó shall assuredly reach such a stage of perfection that we shall be able to have a large hand in punishing Germany for her great sin against humanity, the, fact remains, to the confusion of our lying enemy, and to our lastin credit in the matter of exonei:ation, that while they made preparations for years , before the conflict came upon us, we did not make preparations on a large scale until after- I wards. That is evidence that will satisfy foreign countries and historians as to who were the iggressors and who the defenders in this great test of strength. At the outbreak of the war we heard a great deal from a Germany self-assured of her strength that might is right, and of the world domination which she intended to claim by virtue of her might. That might received somewhat severe handling in Belgium, France and Poland, and the claim became a whine. But subsequent successes, as we say, have brought the real Germany to the front again, and we are able to see what she wants. A secret memorandum which has recently been presented to the CHANCELLOR by six of the most important economic organisations in the country, outlines a policy, which has the sup- port of the German National Liberal party. There is no beating aboo the bush in this programme of German ambition. There must be no, premature peace, the CHANCELLOR is told, until an extension of German power, such as- is outlined, has been assured. In the first place, provision must be made for a colonial empire Which will fully satisfy the numerous economic interests of Germany. Bel- gium -must be taken' as a vis-a-vis to • -the English. Northern France must be held for its iron and coal. Poland is necessary and must be held as an agricultural overflow for the congested Fatherland. The wholesale theft of national territorities is not the .worst feature of this cultured -programme. Not only are . the lands to., be taken, but the dwellers in them are to be outlawed or en- slaved. For instance, with regard to Bel- gium, the memorandum says:

By constituting a Walloon area. and a preponderant Flemish area,. and by placing in German hands the economic enterprises and properties so important for the domina- tion of the country, we shall organise government and administration in such a ,way that the inhabitants will not be able to acquire any influence upon the political destinies of the German Empire'.

As to France.: These territorial increases—as is a matter of course after our experiences in Alsace Lorraine--assume that the population of . the annexed territories will not be -a7tlii to obtain a political influence upon the destinies .of the Gorman Empire, and that all the sources of economic power in these tertitOries, including properties large and small, will pass into German hands. France • will indeNnify the proprietors and absorb them.

. Here .we have in brief a bid for that world- wide domination that was originally advanced and temporarily denied by its authors. The German dream is that, in the first place, Ger- many shall have a Coast line 'in Western Europe that will face ours and challenge our supremacy at sea. There is to be one great sweep here, for twelve months ago the GERMAN FOREIGN SECRETARY announced that "it is obvious that' we could not .profitably annec Belgian territory without making at the same time territorial acquisitions at the expense of Holland." The industrial north of France is to go to the brigands and the agricultural lands of Poland are to go down before that necessity which, in German morals, knows no law. For the working men of England this programme has some special attractions. The oversea trade by which we very largely ,live and prosper is to be annexed. Our colonial markets are to be organised on the German plan. The people of the countries to whom we are allied are to be despoiled and deprirea

of political power. All of us are to be made to-pay indemnities for having dared to defend ourselves, indemnities that would crush and impoverish us for generations. Fortunately we, too, have our programme. Briefly, it is that we will fight to the last man, turn out munitions to the last ounce, and spend our money to the last penny to defeat this con- spiracy against civilisation; and there is, fortunately, good reason to believe that Kultur will succumb before the assaults that will ere long be directed upon it.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CRY FOR COMPULSORY SERVICE.

In common with many other newspapers we have received, we regret to say, a notifi- cation from the National Service Leagu: announcing that they have decided to throw in their lot with the agitators who are making an untimely demand for the introduction of universal and compulsory military service. No more powerfur reply to this agitation could be given than the appeal for unity, which we reproduce, that Lord Hugh Cecil has addressed to these advocates of national ser- vice. He describes as incredible folly a con- troversy which threatens to split the nation in two if persisted in, maintains that the change is undesirable, and, perhaps the most important consideration of all, holds that the dispute they are so light-heartedly entering upon is based upon ignorance. Upon the question of the voluntary versus compulsory system of military service in this country there is a sharp and vital division of opinion. To bring that division of opinion into active operation in the midst of a struggle in which every man and every woman must be united if we are not to succumb would be criminal folly. The war has changed ideas in many directions, and at its close it will be quite legitimate to argue out the pros. and cons. of a new system founded upon experience; but for the present let us cast controversy to the winds. We can do this with all the more peace of mind, because the Government and Lord • Kitchener have knowledge, where the advocates of compulsion are plunging in ignorance. Whatever Lord Kitchener says he wants ho will have, as up to now he has had, without amateur and interested interfer- ence from outsido. lfp to the present moment there have been no signs, even from a corn- pulsionist's point of view, that compulsion, if adopted, would do any good. Men have en- listed voluntarily by the million, men who are in the main the glory and the backbone of the country. The liberal flow of free men has not yet been exhausted, and the supply is quite equal to the requirements of the War Office. Let us put controversy—especially when it is so needless—behind us, and con- tinue to show a united front before which the enemy will assuredly go down.

Last week-end two appeals were made to the people of Warrington for charitable pur- poses largely connected with the war, and, notwithstanding the .many claims that have been made upon the resources of the public, both proved a great success. Gd., or £67 19s. 91d. in all. Local 'Caledonians and the Working Men's Committee are to be congratulated both upon the energy they put into their good work, and the. capital .results that crowned their. efforts. '

THE THRIFT CAMPAIGN.

Owing to the fact that the period of daylight is now rapidly shortening the open-air thrift campaign has been brought to a close. It is considered by the promoters that much good work has been done in making the public realise the extent to which the nation is now relying upon the economical practices of its people. A conference for women will be held in the Town Hall early next month.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
SON OF ALDERMAN FRANCOMB.

Mr. Francomb was the son of the late Alder- man R. W. Francomb, who was Mayor of War- rington 1889-90-1. and died December wo7. He be- longed to the firm of solicitors of Sutton, Elliott, Turnbull, Mayne and Francomb, Spring Gardens, Manchester. In early life he was in the office of the late Mr. W. H. Brook, War- rington, and his articles were transferred to Mr. Henry Greenall. Mr. Francomb had a dis- tinguished scholastic career and was MA. and E.C.L. of Oxford and L.L.B. of Dublin. For many years he was clerk to the Visiting Com- mittee of Winwick Asylum.

Prior to going to Manchester Mr. Francomb was well-known in Warrington as an athlete, particularly as an Association footballer, though he ultimately adopted the Rugby code.

It is a sad coincidence that the late Mr. Frederic Taylor was transferred from Mr. Brook to Mr. Henry Greenall at the same time as the late Mr. Francomb. Mr. Taylor was accidentally shot a few years ago, and now his old colleague has met with a fatal accident.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 28, 1915.

THE CRY FOR "NATIONAL SERVICE.'

Incredible Folly that Threatens Us with New

Divisions.

UNSKILFUL CONTROVERSIALISTS.

An attempt is being made in certain quarters to make the present period of crisis the occasion for introducing National Service, military and industrial, into this country.

The,National Service League has just issued a manifesto, which we reproduce, withdrawing from its participation in the national resolve to bring about unity ,by suspending its propaganda. We also reproduce opinions from the Conserva- tive side, and therefore from sources that will be beyond criticism by our political opponents, lamenting the fact that this attempt to divide the country should be made at the present time.

NATIONAL SERVICE LEAGUE.

the meantime let us trust the judgment of the Government as that of men whose love of country is at least equal to that of any of their critics, and whose knowledge of the true facts of the military situation must be infinitely greater."

===== 0002601_19150828_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
Demand For Universal And Compul- sory Military Service.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,---In the best interests of the country will you kindly find space in your valuablo ,paper for the enclosed. Yours very truly. D. STRACHAN, Major, Secretary.

At the beginning of the war the National Ser- vice League decided to suspend its propaganda, and to place its whole organisation at the dis- posal of the Government. Instructions to the branches of the League to this effect were given on August 7th, 1914, and this policy was re- affirmed in the circular issued in November, 1914, after the lamented death of Lord Roberts. Since then the 'whole situation has changed. The unprecedented intensity of the struggle, the absolute necessity of placing forces of unfore- seen magnitude into the field, and the wide- spread demand throughout the country for a more equitable distribution of the burden of military service, render it necessary to recon- sider the policy adopted under different circum- stances a year ago. ,

_ The existence of the British Empire depends upon success in this war, .and it becomes increas- ingly evident that to obtain success we must put forward our whole strength by establishing universal and compulsory Military service for such period as the war may last. In the opinion of the Council the time has come when all the efforts Of the League should be directed to attaining this end. The question of the best organisation in time of peace may be left/to be decided when peace returns. What we have to do now is to muster under arms all the manhood of the country; of military age, rtot required for, other national services. The necessity of this course -is at last being recognised in many quarters which were formerly averse, from National Service, and it is the duty Of the League to co-operate heartily with the men of all classes and parties who are striving to bring about the fullest development of ihe miJitary capacities of the. nation. (Signed) TVIILNEIt, •Chairnian of 'Council. The. National Service League, 72, Victoria-street, Westminster, S.W.,

"DAILY TELEGRAPH" SAYS . "TRUST THE GOVERNMENT."

The " Daily Telegraph" has a weighty leacl ing article counselling the country to trust the Government and to adhere to the voluntary principle until the time, should it come, when his Majesty's advisers tell us that militdry necessity makes the supreme change imperative. - "We have gone," says the "Telegraph," "through a whole year of War without danger- ous controversy, we have successfully sunk partisanship, we have set up a Coalition Government whose sole raison d'être is to prosecute the war unflinchingly to the triuMphant issue. And yet now we see con- troversialists who are eager to be on the war- path again—not against the common enemy, but against one another."'

===== 0002601_19150828_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
Time For Loyalty, Not Criticism.

The " Telegraph " proceeds. to quote Lord Derby's counsel that this is the time for loyalty to the Government and not for criticism, and that the decision for or against conscription must be left to the Government.

"This agitation," proceeds --t7l-1.; writer, "has. brewed up a most mischievous and stormy atmosphere. Those who advocate compulsory military seriice seem to have no conception of the strength of the old feeling against it. When the people see what they judge to be an at- tempt to stampede the Government their pre- judices at once start into life again. If a sense of public spirit and public duty does not guide the decisions of Mr. Asquith ,and Mr. Balfour, of Lord Lansdowne and Sir Edward Grey, of Mr. Lloyd George and Mr. [Bow Law, of Lord Curzon and Lord Crewe—to _name only a few—where are we to look for n rtif new saviours of the State.?

Lord Kitchener Knows The Facts.

He, at least, is not a politician, and is not moved, con- sciously or unconsciously, by political motives. But he and the Cabinet know the facts at which their outside critics can only make random guesses.

The disturbers of the national unanimity do. the Staten marked dis- serVice when they needlessly provoke contro- versy and create a Most injitrions impression not only at home, but among the allies.
