AN APPEAL FOR UNITY.

Lord Hugh Cecil Supports The Truce.

. The -following letter appeared on Thursday in " The Times ":

• Sit,—l venture to make an appeal for the di;continnance of - the controversy about National Service. Ever since the beginning of the war .there has been a political truce, and in. the list few weeks a Coalition .Government has been formed in order that the nation may proceed with The conduct of the war in, a spirit of absolute union. But there is little advantage in a truce or .coalition. if there is to be a new partisan conflict between pro-compulsionists,and .anti-compulsionists , quite ,as excited as, and much, less intelligent than, the 'normal contro- versies between Liberals and Unionists.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
Incredible Folly.

Having sacrificed .much • for the sake of national unity, it is surely incredible folly to destroy that unity afresh by a .new division. Yet this is what is being done; and all the more speedily ,because the disputants in the new con- troversy are singularly inept in the art of per- suasion. Although it might be supposed that the desirability of a general consent was acknowledged on both sides, those.who take part in the, argument about National Service seem to be ignorant' of the elementary rule that when you are persuading people you should use arguments that' appeal to them rather than arguments. that appeal to yourself. My pre- judice is rather against' National Service, although I should be quite prepared to acquiesce in it in case of necessity,, and I notice accord- ingly .this defect rather in its advocates than in its opponents. I seldom read any plea in favour of National Service without its raising in. my mind a strong hoile that the writer may be disappointed in his object; and I have to remind myself that it is, after all, more im- portant to dtfeet Germany than to thwart those whose reasons seem to me unconvincing, and whose appeals to sentiment seem to me repellent. _ _ _ _ _

Change Undesirable Now.

But this controversy, whether skilfully or un- skilfully conducted; is out of place. Almost every one is agreed, I suppose, that .it would be better to adopt compulsory service than to fail in the object of the war; almost every one again is agreed, I suppose, that it is in itself undesir- able to make a great change in the military organisalion of the, country at such a crisis, if 'it can be avoided. The decision of the question whether we should or should not have universal compulsory National Service turns not on any general principle, but on Whether in all the cir- cumstances of the case such service is expedient. or inexpedient. And about the relevant circum- stances of the case we are all, except the Govern- ment, wholly ignorant. We do not know how many men have joined the Army; we do not know how many men the Government wish to join the Atiny ; we do not know how many men it is possible to equip, nor at what rate. pro- gress in the furnishing of equipment is being made; and again we do not know how many men are wanted for work for munitions of war, and how many ought to be left for agriculture and the different, wealth-producing industries that are necessary for our financial strength. We know, in fact, hardly anything which an intelligent man would take into consideration in deciding a question which, nevertheless, your numerous correspondents and your writers of leading articles insist on discussing. Barren Dispute Based on Ignorance.

The whole dispute is barren and futile to an unprecedented and almost to an incredible degree, because it is based upon ignorance. Or, rather, it is futile but not barren, for it grows a crop of ill-feeling and party bitterness which sprouts abundantly without the fertilisation of knowledge, and excites passions 'which may easily destroy all power of forming a sober and Sane judgment if the Government should pro- pound a policy for the acceptance of their fellow conntrymen. There seems to me only one reason- able attitude in the matter, and that is to say that we will support National Service or any other measure which Lord Kitchener and his col- leagues tell us is necessary; but that, since they have, and we have not, the knowledge which is required to form a just opinion, we leave the determination of the question absolutely in their hands.

There are many exhortations to self-denial being made at the present time, and I may pre- sume to add one. to them. There could scarcely be any healthier act of self-denial than to form a resolution never to utter any word in public either for or against National Service during the continuance of the war. This abstinence would, I believe, be of sensible value to the public interest. and would, I am sure, involve a wholesome though severe effort of self-disci- . . plino on the part of many of your correspond- ents. • •

===== 0002601_19150828_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
I am, yours faithfully, HUGH CECIL. TRADE UNION OPPOSITION.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMAN'S OPPORTUNITY.

The Government's Reason For National Registration.

A FORECAST.

BY HAROLD MAYBURY.

The general impression prevails throughout the country and especially among the working classes and single men of the community that the present Registration Act has been brought into force with a view to the much discussed question of whether or not conscription is to be thrust upon those eligible for military service to the country which gave them birth.

The Secret Of Registration. Prior to the Registration Act one noticed when reading the daily papers, the great num- ber of leading commercial men and political writers who have given their views of why con- scription was to be a thing of the near future. Their contributions, read by vast numbers of the working classes have been duly noted, and the latter have been a the opinion that the writers have had an inner knowledge of the workings of the Government's scheme. The reticence observed by the originators has, it is well-known, puzzled and created a feeling of un- rest and doubt in the minds of ountless thous- ands of men and women in this country of ours. What is it for? That has been the question On the lips of thousands of men, and women occupying positions both humble and exalted, the length and breadth of our land. One already knows of the extreme secrecy observed by those in power, as witness the censoring of news for publication and of the suppression of the real facts of the war at the commencement of hostili- ties. As a furtherance to this has come the secrecy in respect to National Registration. The writer therefore gives a forecast of what the future may hold in store for the masses of people who to-day are working in their ordinary spheres of life and who, moreover, are not being of service to the Motherland in helping to break down the barrier of German militarism which has threatened to destroy civilisation and the nobler purposes of mankind. Why have women between the age of 15 and 65 to register as well as men? Let us look at this as the first point in question and endeavour if possible to hit upon the solution. As everyone knows at the former age both -sexes have to a large extent finished their education as far as that -taught in school is concerned. At this age many' thousands of women have been duly installed either in offices, factories, shops or in prdfessions of minor detail. In consequence of the present crisis there has already opened to the women of England new spheres and con- ditions of life which hitherto were shut to them.

To-day we see women conductors on Corpora- tion tramways, ticket collectbrs, postwomen, munition workers, motor drivers and also in other positions which previously had always been looked upon as the occupations of the opposite sex. Not only so, but it is with pleasure that one notices that they are fulfilling' these duties both satisfactory and well. It is therefore to be seen that there is hardly a vocation which women cannot ably fill with the exception, perhaps, of heavy work, such as is engaged upori in iron foundries, where heat and manual labour is contrary to the physical strength of womanhood.

Men Who Could be Replaced by Women. There are at the present time many hundreds of thousands of men, young, middle-aged and old, whose positions could be filled ably and satisfactorily by the female sex, and what is more, I make the assertion that before very long their places will be filled by them: How many male shop assistants, clerks, insurance agents, sorters, salesmen, tailors, bakers, butchers, and other multitudinous workers in trades and occupations are there who could not be of real andsincere service to this country, whose positions could not ably be filled by the women of our land, who are willing to perform ' these tasks? Is this the reason for he inclusion of women in the Registration Act? Are they to take up these positions? Is man's supremacy as the wage-earner to be a thing of the past, and woman's equality in business and commer- cial enterprise to be a factor of our national life? These are questions to which one may answer "Yes" and not be far wrong. _Possibly to the mind will come the question of whether or not the nation will deteriorate in physical force and morals in consequence, for one may say that life in factories and workshops for women is not conducive to healthy growth and physical wellbeing. The answer is: "There can be no change," for is it not only for a little while, until we as a nation have issued vic- toriously out of the present mad vortex of war? The women of England have proclaimed with loud voices, "Let us help to end this bloody campaign. Let us help our brothers, our hus- bands, our sweethearts, our fathers to end this ghasfly war," and maybe the cry is to ba an- swered in the form of the little squares of paper the women of England have just signed.

Wages Saved for the Nation. Another point due for consideration is, if women are to occupy the positions of thousands of their men folk, will not employers of labour derive a greater profit by the employment of women, and if the Government' take over those men whose positions have been filled by women, who is to pay their wages and where will the money come from? I think we may safely assert that there are indications that the Government will not allow firms to make or accumulate vast profits caused by the lowering of expenditure through the employment of women. Already we have heard of private commercial firms hav- ing made profits which hitherto were undreamt of, and especially by those engaged on Govern- ment work. I know to-day a Lancashire manu- facturer who previous to the war was hardly in a position to make both ends meet, yet at the present moment possesses three motor cars for private use, and all because his factory is en- gaged in the output of war commodities. No, I do not think it will benefit very much employers of labour, and this is how I think the difficulty will be overcome. We will take, for example, the earnings of the working man, which included artisan, labourer, clerk, &c., at an average of 355. or 375. 6d. If a woman takes up this posi- tion at an average of 20s. or 225. 6d., it stands to reason that there would be a vast difference in the wages and expenses of a commercial firm. The surplus of 15s. per person could, I main- tain, be appropriated by the nation to help part pay the wages of the thousands of men who would be engaged in the output of muni- tions and other commodities necessary for a successful conclusion to the present war. In other words, the increased cost of maintaining our army and its workers would partly be borne by the firms in question. I may be wrong in my forecast, but I think this is the reason for the inclusion of females in the Registration Act.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
Women Workers and not 'Conscription.

I make the assertion that it is not for con- scription that this Act has been brought into force. It is with.a view to obtaining a speedier conclusion to the present war and giving an opbortunity to women to be of service in attain- ing this:end. Without doubt.there are thousands of men who could at present be -engaged on munition work. Those engaged in offices could be utilised in clerical work under Government, such as checking stocks, consigning goods and packing, and the surplus could he put to other uses.

I think it is to those, in such occupations as previously mentioned whose services will be requisitioned before long, for they can easily be spared and are an army in themselves. Their occupations are on the light side and therefore suitable to the many thousands of women who are willing and ready to take up these positions. Not only will they be helping to end the present campaign but it will be the means of an additional source of revenue to many thousands of homes, as not only will the sons of parents be wage earners but their daughters as well, who hitherto 3vere merely helping with house- hold duties. t do not say that every daughter will be engaged by the Government, as such a proposition in many cases would be impossible. Then again one will agree that there are a fast number of married women without children dependent upon them, who are skilled in many branches of Government work, women who have been brought up in factory life, women whose husbands are at present fighting for King and country who are willing and anxious to help. Yes, I think the Registration Act can be

Accident to Mr. A. V. Baxter.

While driving his motor cycle along the high- road from Northwich, on Saturday afternoon, Mr. A. V. Baxter, of Waratah Lower Walton, a director of Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., collided at the cross-roads near the Wheat Sheaf Inn with a motor cycle and side-car, be- lieved to have come from Manchester. As a result Mr. Baxter's machine was wrecked and his left Jeg severely injured. Assistance was rendered'by a passing doctor, and Mr. Baxter was afterwards conveyed in an ambulance to Dr. Fox's nursing home at' Warrington, where he is at present being treated. The rider of the other machine received a severe shaking, while! a lady who was riding in the side-car was thrown out, but fortunately escaped serious injury. Child Fatally Scalded.

At Warrington on Saturday, Coroner S. Brighouse held an inquest on the body of Ellen Clough, aged fifteen months, daughter of Mary Clough, 61, Back Aikin-street.—Evidence was given to the effect that on Monday of last week some pigs were being killed in the yard at the back of the house. There was a copper full of boiling water in the house, and a man named Frank Ellison, of 43 Aikin-street, was carry- ing the water from the boiler to the yard for the purpose of scalding the meat which had been killed. While carrying a bucket full of the water the child came behind him unnoticed. He backed against her, with the result that some of the water splashed on to IYer face. She was treated as an outpatient at the Infirmary, but died on Thursday morning of 'last week.— The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death?'

Leather Market Steadily Good. Business in this district continued to run very strongly, production was very heavy, and, with this and the output running on parallel lines, trade moved to its fullest capacity throughout the week (writes the local corre- spondent of the "Leather Trades Review "). Everything is approaching a standard, produc- tion is at its highest, demand is constant and both hide and leather prices are, to an extent, regulated, so that it becomes a little difficult to pick out items of note. The leather market continues steadily good, but at the moment scarcely seems to afford signs of further expan. sion. Buyers of Army goods are mostly con- cerned in getting deliveries of contracted lines, and at the moment new business is not much in evidence, but as forward supplies are fairly closely booked up for three or four months ahead this is not a serious matter, except in so far as it may regulate the particular class of hide to work in. English bends of all weights have been in full demand, and there is evi- dently much difficulty in finding a sufficient supply of the lighter weights. Foreign bends, too, are sought eagerly. Shoulders are both strong and active, as the heavy present demand is backed up by a steady inquiry for future supplies, and already most tanners find them- selves committed to as much as they are likely to produce this year.

"Am I My Brother's keeper ? "

At Sunday's meeting of the WycliffeP.S.A.— over which Mr. W. Beswick presided—a forcible address was given bir Mr. J. Travis, of Widnes, upon "Am I My Brother's Keeper?" Plenty of men (said the speaker), whose lives were governed by greed and selfishness, said each max must look to himself alone. Such unscrupulous men we found in business—men, for instance, ivho trafficked in drink, although they knew plerfectly well how injurious it was to their fellow creatures, and although they saw its bail effect upon the troops all over the country. Away in the trenches the drink could be kept from the men; but at home it could not. For there were men who did not think they were their brother's keeper who "treated"ethem and encouraged them in their evil habit. But the man who realised that he • was his brother's keeper would deny himself for his brother's good—even for the nation's good. The Govern- ment had done a little, but might do much more.—The Secretary read from Mr. Hodson a letter returning thanks for sympathy, and also welcomed two brethren home on furlough— Lance-Corporal C. Spencer and Private Gage". —A vote of sincere sympathy was passed with Captain James Summerfield (of Sankey) anti Mrs. Horsefield (Fleetwood) in the respective loss of their son and daughter, Mr. and Mrs. T. Summerfield. who had lost their lives on war service at sea. The captain and mate were both , former members of the Society.

PENKETH TRAGEDY.

Young Lady Knocked Down Whilst Alighting From Motor.

There wa.s a tragic occurrence in Sankey os Thursday night. Miss Kearns, who lived with her brother and younger sister at Gonfa, Liver- pool-road, Sankey, was alighting opposite her home from a friend's car, shortly after half - past eight, when a _motor waggon owned by Mr. Powell, florist, of Warrington, knocked her down, rendering her unconscious. The unfor- tunate young lady was taken into her house. and Dr. Murray, of Penketh, was called in. - Despite his unremitting attention, however, death ensued about half-past one in the morn- ing, without any recovery from unconsciousness taking place._

It is stated that the waggon was travelling very slowly, and pulled up within five yards. The waggon did not run over the young lady. and no limbs were broken. Much sympathy is felt with the bereaved brother and sister.

S. Lancs. Prisoners Of War Fund.

Up to the present time it has been found possible by the generous support received frozn Regimental and other sources to send weeklY parcels, value about 35., to all known prisoners of this regiment, but it is feared that unless the subscriptions are more rapidly forthcoming the men may not get them so frequently. Thee is no charge for postage on parcels up to ill weight, and every penny contributed is °see for the benefit of prisoners. Acknowledgments received from the prisoners show grateful appreciation of what is being done for them. The call is urgent, as the numbers are dailf increasing. Will you help? S. LANCS. AT THE FRONT.

Captain Boast's Budget Of

News,

THANKS FOR GIFTS.

The Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle) has received the following letter, which will be read with the deepest interest : Dear Sir,—l haven't written to you for some time, and as I, know our many friends and admirers are always glad to hear some news of us I thought I might just fake the opportunity of writing, to you while we are enjoying a short rest. Since last writing to you we have moved about in different parts of our line. For some time we held an important part of the salient east of the once very fine Belgian town, which has been mentioned so often, and which is now famous in the, history of the present war.

SEcOND SOUTH LANCS AND "TERRIERS"

WORK TOGETHER.

"We have also held important sectors south of that place, and latterly we have been in the trenches, with a brigade of the new Army. Recently we'have returned to our own brigade and are now enjoying a well earned rest prior to returning to the trenches for another turn of duty. For a couple of Months the battalion was employed on special work in connection with general disposition of our division, and latterly this work has been taken over by our local Territorial Battalion, which, as you know, forms a unit of our brigade. "We are not permitted to mention our actual whereabouts, but at the present we are 'some- where not actually in France.' Every one is thoroughly enjoying the rest which we are now having, and every day we get up ,some form of amusement for the benefit of the men. A series of sports .have been in progress during the last couple of days and such events as wrestling on horseback, games of football, the players being in sacks, bare-back horse races and such like, have caused endless amusement to The spirit of the .men is really wonderful. _ _ . . .

, Our last turn in the trenches was more than twice the normal period, and the sector actually held was a most trying one, our casualties being rather heavy, but in spite of all, the cheerfulness of the men was splendid, and after a ,very short rest their troubles are soon forgotten. I mention this as I feel sure it will give satisfaction to those at home who are so much interested in the various members of the battalion. "In addition to the two battalions of the regiment serving twether in this brigade, there are two others out here, but we have not yet had the luck to drop across them, and of course there is another battalion of the regiment taking an active part 'somewhere else.'

"The men of the battalion all very much appreciate the gifts of smokes. milk and ether luxuries sent .out by Colonel Blackburne, your- self and other kind friends—and I get quite a atumber of newspapers from different people, which I distribute amongst the men.

"I cannot tell you any actual win' news—hut, of course, you get all that from the daily papers. Still as I know you like an occasional letter from the battalion, I thought I might take this opportunity of writing to you.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
A NEW COLOR/EL

"Colonel Dudgeon, who commanded ns for come 'time, has recently been promoted to the eommand of an infantry brigade—a fact which we are all very proud of—and we are now commanded by an officer who served quite recently at the depit, and who is no doubt well remembero by many local people—l refer to Major If. T. Cotton, who has been given the rank of Lieutenant-Colonel since assuming command of the battalion.. It is regretted tha.t several of our senior officers are away from the battalion in consequence of sickness and wounds, but we hope to have some of thorn back with us. soon. *" In conclusion I. must again thank you for your kind interest in the battalion and ask you to convey our kind regards to all old friends. "Yours very sincerely, "SYDNEY T. BOAST. " Capt. 2nd Batt. S. Lanes. Regt."

COMMISSIONS AND PROMOTIONS.

In Monday' night's ." London Gazette," the following announcements were made Mr. Philip • Carlisle Garnett, Brookside, Penketh, received a commission as second- lieutimant in the 4th West Lancashire Howitzer Brigade. Mr. Garnett is a son of the late %fr. Robert Garnett, and Mrs. Garnett, who is well-known in Warrington and district, having formerly resided at Hall Nook, Penketh. Tie is a member of the firm of Messrs. Robert Garnett and Sons, of Warrington and Liver- pool, and is also a member of the Warrington Board of Guardians, and represents the rural 4listrict of Penketh.

Private H. Dolan, sth Battalion the King's 'Own (Royal Lancaster) Regiment also received a second-lieutenancy. Mr. Dolan was, before enlisting, employed by Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons. He is a son of Mr. Joseph Dolan, builder, Warrington. South Lancashire Commission.

ri. Broome, of the Prince of Wales' Volun- teers (South Lancashire Regiment), has been appointed temporary second-lieutenant.'

Territorial Promotions.

In the 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regi- 2nent the following promotions are gazetted -C. D. Smith, Cadet J. Erskine, from the Man- chester University Cont. Senior Division 0.T.C., and J. Blookes and N. E. Roy Silcock, to be second-lieutenants.

THE SEEDS OF WAR.

Let Us Beware What We Teach Our

Children.

"What are the seeds of war?" asked Mr. W. Tr. Sherburn, in closing the thrift meeting at the foot of Lovely-lane on Thursday evlining. 'Suspicion, misunderstanding, hatred and iolence are the seeds of war," he continued, and let us try our best to prevent these seeds being Sown in the minds of our children. Car- lyle has truly said: If you start erecting a build- on a crooked foundation, the higher the building becomes the more crooked it grows."

===== 0002601_19150828_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
MILITARY WEEK!

Great Celebrations To Take

Place.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
STRIKING SPECTACLES.

One of the biggest—if not the biggest—mili- tary efforts yet made has been planned for the special benefit of Warrington and district; and will take place during the week commencing September 6th. The town it will take by storm—we. are likely to hear of little else that week: for—based upon co-operation between the military and civilian population—it will spread out its tentacles to practically every sort of place. This special movement, which is in connec- tion with Lord Derby's great recruiting cam- paign for the West Lancashire Territorial . .

-Force Association, is to have centres in Liver- pool, Bolton, Preston, Lancaster and St. Helens, as well as in Warrington. But in none of these places is it possible for a finer programme to be put forward than has been arranged for the benefit of this town and district. For one thing a glance at the list of organisers is sufficient guarantee of this—Captain R. M. Ma.thams (commanding administrative )centre 4th South Lancashire Regiment) and Captain Eric Fair- dough, with the assistance of the political agents, Messrs. E. A. Sanders and H. Hayes, and other gentlemen. Captain Toivnroe (who will visit the town on behalf 43. f the Territorial Association) is also taking a leading part throughout the organisation of the campaign.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
THREE FAMOUS BANDS.

For the purpose of rousing all and sundry, two famous bands have been secured—Lord Derby's . Recruiting Band and the Liverpool Scottish Pipers. One of the Corporation motor- buses will be used for the conveyance of the band to the outlying districts; and there will be an illuminated tramcar, in which wounded soldiers Will ride. '

'By kind permission of Colonel Moggridge and officers at Orford Barracks, the Band of the 2nd South 'Lancashires will also a....4sist during the week. ,

Open-air meetings will also be held through- out the week in the locality. These will in- clude mid-7day gatherings at the different works, short meetings every evening at the places of amusements, and spectacular route marches in the town and neighbourhood. The following will be the local open-air, meetings:— Monday, September 6th, Bridge Foot. Tuesday, The Rodney, Winwick-street. Wednesday, Corner of Green-street and Lovely-lane. Thursday, Round Parish Church Lamp. Friday, Victoria-square, Stockton Heath. Saturday; Cornhill. The outlying districts meetings cover ground on a correspondingly large scale, and. are : Monday, September 6th, Go'borne and Hay- dock. Tuesday, Ashton and Bryn.n. Wednesday, Lowton, Kenyon and Culcheth. Thursday, Sankey, Penketh and Bold.

Friday, -"Winwiek", -Croft and Padgate. Saturday, • Newton and Earlastown.

SOME WELL-KNOWN SPEAKERS.

The speakers will include : Mr. Will Crooks, M.P., Captain Townroe, Messrs. S. Thompson, G. L. Payton, G. 11. BibbingS, J. W. Taylor and W. Tonybee, Councillor Stevenson, Messrs. S. Ja.mison and Coleman (of the Imperial Mari- time League), Mr. .P. L. Joseph, Sergeant Morrison and others.

'Among local 'celebrities who have promised their assistance as chairmen of meetings and in-'other capacities are: Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall, Messrs. C. E. Parker. W. Peter _yylands, F. W. Monks and J. Fulton-Smith, and Alderman Tinnion.

The Preparatory Training Corps and Boy Scouts, it is hoped, will take part. It is desired that the Territorial Association, in accordance with their arrangements in Other towns, will meet various representative bodies and trades organisations, with a view to discuss- ing recruiting methods, particularly for the 3/4 South Lancashire Regiment.

This arrangement has been made with much success in those towns where it has already been tried, and no doubt the business men of Warrington will meet the moment's needs in equally satisfactory fashion.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CINEMA VAN.

Mr. J. S. Arkwright's Cinema Van, which has for many months been engaged upon such valuable recruiting work all over the country is also coming. Elaborate arrangements, it may be mentioned, have been made for the attesta- tion, medical examination and immediate cloth- ing of recruits.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCASHIRE MEN FOR LANCA- SHIRE BATTALIONS.

Although at_a, time like the present it makes no ultimate difference to the nation into .which regiment a man enlists, yet to the Lancashire man Lancashire battalions should have a par- ticular appeal. This is especially the case if a man joins the Territorial units. But whether in the Territorial or the Regular Army a Lancashire man in one of the famous, regi- ments will . find comradeship and esprit de corps. More important than tis, from the family point- of view, it is probable that he will be trained in the elementary stages at some camp not too far away from his home. This represents direct saving of money, for relatives are spending large sums of money on railway fares and expenses in visiting their ,sons, sweethearts, or husbands in training camps. In the case of Lancashire Territorial units, East Lancashire units of the 3rd Line are being trained at Southport 'and West Lanca- shire units. at Weston, a beautiful' camping ground some seven miles from Blackpool. But should' a Man go' to the Front' and there have the misfortune to he wounded or be ill, again the advantage to a 'Lancashire man is apparent, for Lord Derby has made arrange- ments for all convalescents in Lancashire regi- ments to proceed to the County Convalescent Home, Blackpool, which is established in the grand-stands of the Blackpool Racecourse. There have been many complaints in the past from relatives as to the expenses which have been entailed by visits to Convalescent Homes in distant parts of the country, but when this :Blackpool Convalescent Home is in full work- ing order there will be no further reason for grumbling.

PRIVATE WILLIAM SMITH. Killed.

the same regiment, Private William Smith, of the Warrington Territorials—and also one of the five soldier sons of Mr. and Mrs. Smith, of 18, Ellen-street—was killed by a wall falling upon him. Another man also was killed, whilst three were wounded. This news was conveyed in a letter sent by a comrade—Private Shepherd, and later was confirmed by Private John Smith—the eldest brother—who .wrote home saying he had been to See William buried.

Private Smith who was a Married man, and lived at 24, Forster-street, took part in the gr6at charge on June 16th, and came through un- scathed.. He had served the necessary term In the Territorials, and rejoined on the outbreak of war as a member of B Company. As a boy he attended Wycliffe Day School. Formerly employed at Bewsey Forge by .Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, Ltd., he was a member of the Ancient Order of Ocklfellows.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT KILLED.

ticyful News Received By Warrington Parents.

Mr. Bennett, 75, Pierpoint-street, several weeks ago received the official notification from the War Office that his son, Private Ernest Bennett, 6th Battalion South Lancashire Regi- ment, had been killed in action at the Dardanelles on July 16. A letter subsequently received from Private Bennett, and dated July 18th, seemed to suggest that there might have been some mistake, and there has been great anxiety at home till yesterday (Friday, August 27th), when a letter came to hand from Private Bennett, dated August 2nd, and saying not only'that,he was in the best of health, but that he was in a party that had been left behind the firing4ine. The letter says "Our Battalion have again left for the firing- line after a few days' rest. So many out of the Battalion have been left behind, and I PTE. E. BENNETT. have been one of the

_ lucky ones this time: The last time we came out of the trenches we had a lively time. We were getting shelled all the time till our guns found them. We did not hear Much of them after. I have not seen anything of our 'Bill' yet.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
A Sweeter Home-coming.

"1 can't tell you much in a letter. I only wish I was home and then I could tell you a great deal; but never mind, the longer we .are away the .sweeter will be the home-coming. It does one good to get news from your own, and you must let Me know how they are all going on. I happen to. have a cold as I am writing this; I suppose one of our fellows must have left the gate open before going to bed—you know we have only the sky for a roof. Good night. and God bless you all." Private Bennett, who is 31 years of age, worked at the Bewsey Wire Ropery for seven- teen years. As a boy, he attended the Wycliffe School. At one time he was a member of the St. Paul's Young Men's Bible Class. " Our Bill" is his brother, who is in the R.A.M.C. , Naturally Private Bennett's parents, are full of joy at the receipt of this letter, which has put a happy end to their suspense.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 28, 1915.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
ON HONOUR'S SCROLL.

KILLED.

PRIVATE JOHN WILCOCK.

Killed Instantly By Shell In Support

Trench.

Private John Wilcock, of the 2nd South Lancashires, has given his life at the Front. The news first reached his wife at 13, Arnold- street (off Manchester-road) in the following letter from a comrade:—

It is my painful duty to have to inform you of your' husband, Jack Wilcock, getting killed on the 28th July. How he was killed: At about 8.30 on the date mentioned the Germans tried to blow ,our fire-trench up by setting one of their mines off; and afterwards started to shell our support:- trench, which your husband was in, and one of the shells caught the back of the trench, and a piece of the shell caught your husband in the body and killed him in- stantly. Hoping you will take my sym- pathy on your great loss of your husband— who was a Pal of mine, and I will miss him very much.—l remain, yours truly, Private Stringer.

After receiving this letter on August Ist, Mrs. Wilcock had inquiries made in all quarters, but without learning anything further Early this week, however, the official intima- tion arrived to confirm the death.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
CORPORATION ELECTRICIAN.

Aged 38, Private J. Wilcock was a very highly- valued member of the fitting department of the Corporation Electricity Works (where he had worked nine years), and had also lived all, his life at 13, Arnold-street, where he was born. As a boy he attended the National Day Sehokil and the Cockhedge (Wycliffe) Mission Sunday School. He was an enthusiastic bowler, and had won sevpral prizes. Towards the end of November be answered the call, joining the Army. Three months afterwards he was drafted out to the Front, since which time he has written home many interesting letters, one or two of ,which the "Examiner" has had the privilege of publishing. One of his experiences near Hill 60 was that of being blown up into the air and knocked un- conscious, at the same time being cut across the forehead; whilst the very last letter he wrote

SERGEANT HOWARD DAVIS. Killed.

pack and received a severe kick in the lower part of the chest, which put him in bed on slops for some-weeks. He persuaded his parents to let him play again, and his school days passed without further injury. During this period he had the pleasure of scoring 14 tries. in one match, the team winning by 120 points. Ile never: wished to play in such another game. At 16 years of age he joined the Old Edwardians and secured a place it the first team during his second season. Not much chance of county honours came his way as the team were not sufficiently powerful to attract attention. Later he joined the Coventry Club. as he could not afford to be a strict amateur at the time. lin never regretted the change, Northern Union football being, in his ()Pinion, infinitely superior to the Rugby Union code. During three seasons with Coventry Howard Davis played agaiust every team in the Northern Union League, including Warrington.

DIED OF WOUNDS.

PRIVATE J. MURPHY.

A Hero's Death After Hand-to-Hand Fight.

. Private John Murphy, of the Second Lanca- shire Fusiliers, and son of Mrs. Catherine Murphy, 9, Naylor-street, Warrington, has died of wounds received in Belgium. He was 28 sears of age, and at the time war broke out was working at Mr. Melbourne's foundry, Wilders pool Causeway. ," A brave lad," as stated in a comrade's letter given below, he enlisted at the first call of his country for help. While in training in England he played football for his ,regiment, and was also fond of bowling. As a boy he attended St. Mary's School, Butter- market-street.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
A COMRADE'S TRIBUTE.

Private Quinn, of the same regiment, writing to Mrs. Murphy, Augutt 19, said : "I ant sorry to hear that poor John died of his wounds received. in action early in July in the slight at first. We were very close 'chums since leaving England together last October. "You asked me to give you some account of how John was wounded. I am sorry I am not permitted to say much of what goes on out here, but I don't think I am going out of my place in telling you that he had been engaged as a bomb thrower since he came out. It was his duty to fling these bombs into the enemy when at close quarters.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
Got At Close Quarters

" It happened that in this action our bomb throwers got so close to the Germans tliht it came to a hand to hand fight, and it was while throwing a bomb over the Germans' trench that John was hit by one of theirs. He was as brave a lad as ever came out here. He never knew what fear meant, and the night that they went to the trenches for this action we passed the night together at a concert. Poor John seemed to have an omen that he would get wounded.. He said he was going very, close to them and would let., them have a few bombs whatever happened. "As wits my custom I went to look for him when the regiment came out of the trenches,

Died of Wounds.

was first received by his wife at 27, Napier- street, ten days 'ago in a letter from a comrade —Rifleman Tom Burns (of Friars' Green). This writer said he was very sorry to have to 'tell Mrs. Langley that her husband had died oil August 3rd of shrapnel wounds in the stomach. There were only two of the 'War- rington boys in their brigade now left—Rifle- man W. Ashton; who had had his left arin blown off, and Rifleman Thomas Readon. A week later arrived the official notification. Quarter-Century In Army.

Rifleman Langley, whose age was 36, was a native of Warrington, and in boyhood attended People's College. his father was a stonemason at the Ship ,Canal, and upon leaving school the Rifleman went to work under him. Later hp joined the 16th Rifle Brigade, in which he served thirteen years—practically all abroad in India. He then transferred to the Ist South LanCashires, and with these remained eleven years—during which he fought through the South African War, gaining two medals. Afterwards he came home to Warrington, obtaining employment on Messrs. Crosfield's wharf.t Three years later he went to Messrs. It. A. 'Naylor's lay-bye, but in six weeks the war broke out and he answered the call by joining the Rifle Brigade, leaving for the Front in .the middle of February. Besides his wife he leaves a small boy and girl.

SECOND-LIEUT. L. B. TODD.

That well-known Northern Union player, Lancelot' B. Tedd, who, after touring this country with the professional All Blacks, joined the ranks of the Wigan Club, and later played for Dewsbury, has been promoted to Second-Lieutenant in the New Zealand Con- tingent.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
WOUNDED.

PRIVATE THEODORE ECCLESTON.

Hopes To Be Home For Good By Christmas.

News has reached Mrs. Theo. Eccleston, at 16, Arthur-street, that her husband, Private Theodore Eccleston, of the 6th Border Regiment, has been wounded during the fighting in the vicinity of the Dardanelles. The news was con- veyed in a letter from Private Eccleston him- self, which ran as follows : - " Just a line to let you know I am still iii the land of the living, but I am sorry to tell you that I am in hospital suffering from a bullet wound in the left foot, but it is not serious, and I am hoping to be all right in a few weeks. We have had an awful time, and most of our lads got a cutting up.' This is my second time in the firing line. We got eight days of it at first, but it 'was a picnic compared" with the second time. I hope to have my Christmas dinner at home, because I don't think the war can last much longer up here, because we are making great headway!' Private Eccleston, who is thirty-two years or

PRIVATE THEO._ ECCLESTON.

Wounded

age, enlisted shortly after the outbreak of war, having previously been employed by Messrs. Pearson and Knowles at the Dallam Forge. As a boy he attended Holy Trinity day school. He was fond of football, having played several times for the works team. His other brother, Private Thomas Eccleston, is in training with the Army Service Corps.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
SERGEANT-MAJOR A. LEps.

Warrington D.C.M. Wounded At The

Dardanelles.

Sergeant-Major Lees has been wounded while serving in the Dardanelles. • A shell burst and, though the shrapnel killed an orderly standing behind him, the Sergeant-Major himself was fortunate enough to receive only a slight wound in nip left hand, accompanied by some bruising of the ribs. While walking away to receive medical attention he was again hit, but again the consequences were not very serious. Writing from the base hospital at Alex- andria, Scrant-Major -Lees expresses the-hope that he may soon- be in the firing line again. He praises the Australians highly. The latter took some trenches in fine style, and it was while his regiment was following up th•it 1 gallant piece of work that he received his in- juries. The news that Regimental-Sergeant-Major Alfred Lees, of the 6th King's Own Royal Lancasters has been wounded at the Darda.nellea first reached his wife at 2, Alexandra-street, Warrington, by official intimation, on Wednes- day of last week. Sergeant-Major Lees is numbered amongst the town's distinguished soldiers ' because he holds the D.C.M. For over 21 years he served in the King's Liverpool Regiment—which for several years preceding the transfer to Seaforth, was stationed at Orford Barracks. He served throughout the South African War, in which he gained not only the D.C.lf., but also the King Edward and Queen Victoria medals; the good conduct medal he holds as well. On the outbreak of the great war the Sergeant-Major was on pension; but rejoined the Army and early in June went out to the Dardanelles. In other circles he at one time belonged to a Warrington hockey club.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE DAN. TAYLOR. Bewsey Forgeman Wounded At Dardanelles.

Private Daniel Taylor—of the 6th South Lancashires—writes home to his wife at 30,' Foundry-street, to say "I an in the very best of health. I am glad to tell you that my foot is going on all right, but I wish I was at home with it; it would go on a lot better. . . . They say that we will get a furlough when we get right. And I don't think it will be long before I am right; but they don't know whet- ther there are any bones broken or not—l 4on't think there are. I have got to go under X-rays to-day. "I am glad to say I have got back to Eng- land and I don't want to leave it again, for it is Amrder out there. 1 came back on the biggest boat that is 'sailing. . . . It is a nice Iliac° here, and we can't grumble at the food for it is good." Wycliffe Schoolboy.

Aged 32, Private D. Taylor is in peace times a forgeman for Messrs. Pearson and Knowles at Bewsey. He attends Wycliffe Congregational P.S.A. As a boy he attended the Wycliffe School. He has four young boys. The Army he joined shortly after the war outbreak, and left for the Dardanelles about tvto months ago.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
SIR ARCHER CROFT.

Mr. J. C. Parr's Son-in-Law Reported

Missing.

Captain Sir Archer Croft, Baronet, who, a few years ago, married Miss Parr, the eldest daughter of Mr. and Mrs. J. Charlton Parr, Grappenhall, is officially reported missing and latest inquiries show that nothing further is known, the hope being that he may. be prisoner of war. Sir Archer Croft, of Lugwardine Court, Here- ford, when the war "broke out, joined the Ist (Service) Battalion Herefordshire Regiment as a private, and offered to raise, a company of 150 men to help to bring up the battalion to strength. The offer was accepted, and the men were forthcoming in a few days. Having had previous service-as an officer in the Shropshire Militia, Sir Archer was soon gazetted to a second-lieutenancy. This was little more than a fortnight after he had joined in August. In exactly three months from the date of his attestation he was promoted captain while the regiment was in training in Northampton, and with that frank sailed with the let Battalion On July 14th last.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. GORDON lIEWART'S SON KILLED.

Second-Lieutenant Gordon Morley Hewart, who was killed in action at the Dardanelles between the 7th and the 11th August, was the elder son of Mr. Gordon Hewart, K.C., M.P. for Leicester. He was 22 years of age on the 6th August, and was educated at Manchester Gram- mar School, where he was head of the school and captain of games, and at Balliol College, Oxford, where he was elected to a Major Classical Exhibition in 1912. In the spring of last year he took a brilliant first in Honour Classical Moderations. At Oxford, as at school, he was a strenuous member of the Officers' Training Corps and in November last he re-. ceived a commission in the 6th Battalion Lincolnshire Regiment, • ..

===== 0002601_19150828_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
HONOURED BY KING.

SEVEN IN KHAKI.

King's Congratulations To Lythgoe's-lane Parents.

Four sons and three sons-in-law of Mr. and,- Mrs. James Savage, of 45, Lythgoes-lane, are all serving with the Warrington Territorials, and all save one are fighting in France. In recognition of this patriotism the King has sent a congratulatory message to Mr. and Mrs. Savage. The letter, which is signed by Sir Frederick Ponsonby, Keeper of the Privy Purse, runs as follows: " I am commanded by the King to convey to you an expression of His Majesty's appreciation of the patriotic spirit which has prompted your four sons and three sons-in-law to give their services at the present time to the Army. The King was much gratified to hear of the manner in which they have so readily responded to the call of their Sovereign and their country, and I am to express to you, and to., them, Hie Majesty's congratulations on having contri- buted in so full a measure to the great cause for which all the people of the British Empire are so bravely fighting." Mr. and Mrs. Savage's four sons are all married. Private John Savage, the eldest son, is thirty-nine years of age. He made an effort to "join up" soon after the outbreak, but wan rejected on account of bad teeth, whereupon he had all his teeth extracted, and upon trying a second time he was accepted. As a boy he at- tended St. James' Day School, and was formerly employed at the Bewsey Forge by Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, Ltd. ' In peace times he resides at Slater-street, Latchford. Private Daniel Savage, who is thirty-eight years of age, served in the old Volunteers, and joined the " Terriers " at the outbreak of war. He attended St. James' School, and was for- merly employed by the Warrington- Corpora- tion. He makes his home at 15, Norman-street. Private James Savage, who lives in Hale- street, is twenty-nine years of age. He enlisted soon after the outbreak of war, previous be which he was employed at Walker's Brewery. He was educated at St. Ann's School. Private Hamlet Savage was, previous to the outbreak of war, employed as a clerk by the Warrington Corporation. He also was educated at St. Ann's Schobl, and is twenty-five years of age. His home is in Winwick-road. The three sons-in-law are Private Edwin Wellings, of West-street, who is thirty years of age, and was employed by kMessrs. Pearson and Knowles at the Bewsey Forge. Private John Lythgoe, of Earle-street, who in thirty years of age, and was formerly employed at Bewsey Forge. Private Harry Kirk, whose home address is 9. Birchall-street, and who was formerly employed by Messrs. Rylands' Bros. Ltd. He is thirty- one years of age. The only one not at the Front, he is performing his duty quite as use- fully, for he is acting as physical drill instruotor for the Warrington Territorials training in the South.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
PERCY-STREET PATRIOTS.

One Son Killed And Three Still Serving.

The four sons of Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Caswell, of 13, Percy-street, have given their services to the country in this war. Private Samuel Caswell', who was a single man, belonged to the Warrington Territorials, but was killed at the beginning of July. He joined the battalion about four years ago. The family, who have lived in the Lovely-lane dis- trict for thirty-one years, are very well known in Warrington. Private John Caswell, of the King's Own Scottish Borderers, has been serving at the Dardanelles, and has been reported missing since June 4th. In peace times he resides with his wife and four cilildren at 3, Wright-street., He enlisted last February, having previously served in the old Volunteers for six years. • Gunner' Thomas Caswell, of the Royal Field Artillery, is a reservist, and has been at the Front since the beginning of the war. He has a total of thirteen years' 'service. He also is married, and lives at 72, Fox-street. Private Walter Caswell, of the 20th llns,sars, was wounded a short time ago, and recently came home on a short leave. He is now is hospital at Scarborough. He has been in the Army for seven years. He is a married Mfl3l. A congratulatory letter from the King, headed Buckingham Palace, August 13th, and signed by Sir Frederick Ponsonby, keeper of the Privy Purse, was received by the parents. It was as follows

Sir,—l am commanded by the King to convey to you an expression of His Majesty's apprecia- tion of the patriotio spirit which has prompted your four sons to give their services to the Army. The King was much gratified to hear of the manner in which they have so readily responded to the call of their Sovereign and their country, and I am to express to you, and to them, His Majesty's congratulations on having contributed in so full a measure to the great cause for which all the people of the British Empire are so bravely fighting.

MILITARY CROSS

=EVEN

===== 0002601_19150828_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
For South Lancashire Second-

Lieutenant.

His Majesty the King has been graciously pleased to confer the Military Cross, in recog- nition of gallantry and distinguished service in the field, upon Second-Lieutenant Arthur Eustace Stevens, 2nd Battalion Prince of Wales' Volunteers (South Lancashire Regi- ment), attached Ist Battalion Hampshire Regi- ment. , The honour is "for conspicuous gallantry in the operations south of Pilkem, on 6th J_uly, 1915, when in charge of the Grenadier platoon of the Hampshire Regiment, he led his men with the greatest courage, was the first man into the German trenches, and used his gren- ades with deadly effect until he was wounded." The "London Gazette" on Wednesday con- tained a long list of awards for striking deeds of gallantry in France and Flanders and in Gallipoli. The Canadian regiments ars well represented among the recipients of the D.S.O. and the Military Cross. Altogether • 896 decorations have been con- ferred.

CORPORAL TOMBS, V.C.

Receives An Honour From The -Emperor—Otßussia.,

Corporal J. Tombs, V.C., Warrington, of the Ist Battalion Liverpool Regiment, has received, ,along with other British V.C.'s, an honour at the hands of the Czar. His Imperial Alajesty the ,Emperor of Russia has been graciously pleased to confer, with the approval of His Majesty the King, many re- 'wards for gallantry and distinguished service in the field by officers and men in the British Army. The Cross of the Order of Saint George, 4th Class, is conferred upon Corporal Tombs.

THE V.C. AT BUCKINGHAM ,PALACE.

Al Grand Time. Corporal Tombs gives, as reported in a Lon- don contemporary, a racy description of his visit to Buckingham Palace on August 11th, to receive the coveted honour of V.C. from the hands of His Majesty. He describes his adven- tures thus: "The King gave me a real hearty hand-grip. None of your society finger-tips. about him. He held my hand, and said, Magnificent! Splendid! How did you do it? .proud of you.' Then he asked me how the wounded were getting on whom I rescued, and seemed to know all about it. He then left go of my hand, and I saw the tears come into his eyes as he saw, an officer come up hobbling on. crutches. Oh! I've had a grand time in town. ===== 0002601_19150828_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN RURAL COUNCIL

Postponement Of Elections.

There was little business for transaction at Tuesday's meeting of the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Council. Mr. Antwis presided. The Clerk read a letter from the Local Government Board, • intimating-that the next statutory elections of County and Borough Councillors,'District Councillors, Guardians and Parish Councillor.; were postponed for a Year, and the terms of office of existing Councillors and Guardians were extended for one year. Until anew register-came into force, any casual vacancy requiring to be filled by election should be filled by choice of the other members of the body.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
TO KEEP DOWN RATES.

Runcorn Guardians SLipport Resolution To County Council.

Mr. C. Linaker, jun., presided at the fort- nightly 'meeting of the Runcorn Board, of Guardians at Dutton on Tuesday.

GRANULATED FOR CURE SUGAR.

The Local Government Board wrote suggest- ing that the Guardians should take into con- sideration the advisability of discontinuing the use of the cube sugar in any case where granu- lated sugar would sufficiently ,answer the pur- pose. The supply of cube sugar available in this country was now extremely limited, owing to the cutting off of the supplies from the Con- tinent. Granulated sugar was precisely the same as that used in the making of cubes.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
THANKS.

Mr. 'Eaton Bretherton wrote thanking the Board for their kind expression of sympathy with him in the loss of his wife.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNTY ECONOMY.

The Macclesfield Union forwarded the follow- ing resolution: "That this Board draw • the attention of the Cheshire County Council to the continually increasing expenditure. in recent years, the demand on the Macclesfield Board of Guardians having been increased from £9,584 for the half-year ended 31st March, 1911, •to .Z 14,331 for the half-year ended 30th September, 1915, and that in view of the great increase in income' tax and other public burdens, caused by the war, this Board petition the Cheshire County Council to' endeavour to reduce as far as possible, at all events during the continuance of the war and for a year after, the expenditure on roads, education, (t.c., and thus cause a much- needed reduction in local rates."

• The Board were asked to support the resolu- tion.

Mr. Woodward said that he did not agree to supporting it. According to the way he under- stood the letter, it asked he County Council to discontinue all expenditure on roads and edu- cation. He would agree that all extra expendi- ture should be avoided, but to discontinue all expenditure would be to land the country in a large expense to bring up the standard again after the -war. He thought they should main- tain efficiency so far as it had been reached, and suspend extra expenditure.

The Chairman and other members said they thought Mr. Woodward had misinterpreted the meaning of the resolution, and. on the resolu- tion being read again Mr. Woodward with- drew his opposition, and it was .unanimously agreed to comply with the Macclesfield Guard- ian? request.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MASTER'S APPLICATION.

'The ,Master wrote stating that he wanted to join the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment, and sought the permission of the Board to answer in the affirmative the question as to whether he was willing to serve with the Regular Forces if called upon to do so. The Board gave their consent.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
INMATES AND VAGRANTS.

The Clerk reported that the inmates for the past two weeks had numbered 125 and 125, as against 135 and 131 for the corresponding weeks of last year. The vagrants had totalled 61 and 73, as against 72 and 69.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
BRAVE RUNCORN MEN.

At Widnes Police Court on Tuesday two young men, named Albert E. Atherton, 133, Mersey-road, and George Robertson; 70 Farn- worth-street, were presented by Councillor D. Lewis (Mayor) with a framed certificate and the silver medal of the Liverpool Shipwreck and Humane Society for saving a little boy named John Bruce from drowning in the Mersey. Superintendent Foster said that on July 16th the boy fell into the river, and was rapidly carried away on a strong tide. Atherton plunged into the ivater without -taking off his clothing, and with great difficulty secured the child. Robertson also entered the water fully clothed, and on reaching Atherton took the child from him and brought him ashore. Atherton was -very much .exhausted, but man- :aged to struggle ,ashore. The Superintendent reminded- the Court that 'Athertorr had previously saved the lives of two children, one in the Mersey and the other in the Manchester Ship Canal. Robertson rescued a boy from the river fifteen years ago. Both men were complimented upon their , gallant conduct.

THIPPEII STATISTICS.

In reply to a query. anent our last week's article upon " Tripper Troubles," we give the following figures : The average number of trippers who visit Lythm and the immediate district during a week is about I,soo—spread over the three days Monday, Wednesday and Saturday. On a Bank Holiday there, are about 4,000 or -5,000 visitors. The instance of Warburton: Ordinary week, about 400; Bank Holiday, 1,200.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTOR ACCIDENT AT THEL WALL.

Manchester Man Receives Thigh Injury And Shock.

An accident occurred at Thelwall early on Sunday afternoon, When Joseph Kelly, of 76, Junction-street, Ancoats, 'Manchester (who was riding from Grappenhall towards Lyram on a bicycle) collided with a motor car driven by Mr. Harold Matthew Whittaker, engineer, of Norton House, Hornby-road, Old Trafford. , Kelly received a flesh-wound in his thigh, and also suffered from shock. The owner of the motor at once conveyed him to Lymm Red- Cross Hospital, where he was attended to by Dr. Burrowes. Subse- quently Mr. Whittaker took him to Ancoats Hospital.

MGR LE.611 FORESTERS.

Three Of Oldest Members Pass Away In Less Than A Week.

The Court " Cornwall " No. 4 of the Knuts- ford District- Foresters Friendly Society (High Legh), of which Mr. E. Simpson is secretary, suffered a triple and heavy loss last week in the deaths of three• of its oldest members— whose united, ages totalled no less than 235 years. These three members, who passed away in less than a week, were: Mr. Thomas Rollinsen (81), Mr. Thomas_Rinzy (79) and Mr. Richard Shakeshaft .(75). In the club's early clays the last two named worked together in official capacities—the one as chairman and the other as secretary—and, by their labours in the best interests of the locality, • achieved gratifying results. • Strange to say, theY both died on the same clay. - In our last issue we reported the death of Mr. Rollinson, and the fine tribute paid to his fifty years' service by the Hon. J. E. Cross, at a meeting of the Bucklow Rural Council.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
LITTLE GIRL IN CANAL.

Her Life Probably Saved By Boy's Presence Of Mind.

Some. excitement arose in Statham on Mon- day morning; when a little ~,virLnamed•Marjorie Inglefield, of Barrsbank Cottages, fell into the canal. She is about eight years old. It. appears that at the time she was playing with a little boy named Riley, and another boy named. Harold Wrench was sitting on a wall near by. He saw her, .when throwing some- thin.' into the, water, overbalance and fall in; and 'had the presence of mind to run in his home at the front door instead of the back. Mrs. Wrench, on reaching the canal-bank, saw •the girl floating on her back, and throwing herself down stretched out her arm. The girl managed to grasp her finger, and Was thus pulled to safety. After the taking of due precautions, the girl is none the worse for her experience. Much credit attaches to the action of the boy Wrench, for bad he run to the back door of /his home (as he usually did) help would probably have been summoned too late. The incident was witnessed by two or three gardeners working near.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
RED CROSS CONCERT.

Hippodrome _Artistes'_ Afternoon Nisi t

To Drill Hall.

On Thursday afternoon much interest centred in the Drill Hail, for there the wounded soldiers from the lied Cross Hospital were given a good concert. They attended in good force, and the audience was much added to by _the free admittance of civilians.

The concert which consisted of the Hippo- drome programme, as reported elsewhere, but slightly Curtailed, and which lasted from 2.30 to 4.15, was also attended by the Belgians fromi " Beechwood," who, like everybody else, voted it "capital;" • :Unfortunately, :the men from the ,Thelwall Heys. Home .were unable to accept the invitation owing to their Pound Day -bbing that day. •s-• Voluntary contributions were taken to meet the Red Cross expenses. The organisation. was in the hands of Miss Saville, of Lymm, who only three weeks ago planned a similar concert in the Hospital itself, and hopes to see others held. The soldiers enjoy the novelty thoroughly—as Dr. Burrowes (of the hospital) remarked in thanking the artistes.

Private Brookes—who lives at Heald Green, Cheadle, with his wife—is 25 years of age, and was born and brought up in Lymm. Aftei leav- ing school he commenced work as a fustian- cutter, and later went to Mr.- William Long's tanyard, Grappenhall. Here he remained for sortie years, next going as a gardener to Lymm Hall, and later as head gardener for Mr. Champ —with whom be afterwards • proceeded to .Cheadle. Whilst in Lymm he had joined the Territorial movement, and had served one year when the war broke out, and he was called up from his work. After seven months' training he left for the Front: and we have since had ,the pleasure of printing many of his interesting letters home. ,Only four weeks' ago he had a few days' furlough —during which he visited Lymm and saw all his friends.

•.Whilst in Lymm- he belonged to the Primitive Methodist Sunday school. He is also a good member of the Lymm Liberal AsSociatioh, to which he has belonged for about five years. tI \ ATE S. C. PERCIVAL.

Gardener ‘Vounded After Two Weeks Abroad.

Another Lymm soldier, the news of whose wounding has been received this week, is Private Samuel Cheetham Percival, of the 84th Brigade, 2nd Cheshires (formerly of the 3rd). The fact was contained in a letter from his brother, Private, Fred Percival, whom he met in France. • This letter was received, on Thurs- day morning, and said he was slightly wounded, the bullet had scarred his face, but had other- wise missed him. But for the presence of some sand-bags the bullet would have gone through his shoulder.

Aged 28, Private S. C. Percival lives with his wife and little girl- at 7, Moston-street. He was born in Lymm, and after leaving the School went to Work as a fustian-cutter for the late Mr. F. 0. Moston. • lip to his enlistment in May, however, he was employed as a gardener by Mr. Smith of Massey Brook, Thelwall. After training he left- for the Continent only a fortnight ago. The Pri7ate belongs to the P4riSh Church.

LYIANI TERRITORIALS.

"STILL ILIVINC IT QUIET, DESPITE

A FEW SHELLS."

The •Lymm "sth Cheshires " at the Front; have not made any change this week from the ipoiition they took up in the support trenches.

Killed One Man.

Writing on Friday last to Miss B. Cross, Bridgewater-street, Private, P. Clare says; "We are still having it very quiet yet. They sent a few shells over the other day, which just went over our place and landed in a-wood behind us. Sorry to say that, they killed one .man; that is our first loss since we came down here.. . We are having pretty 'fair, weather; we have not had much rain, and can't grumble. . . ." The Lymm " lads " sick and w,-)unded, have got surprisingly scattered. With the new Northwich and Lyium Territorial recruits at Oswestry • there are Lance-Corporals S. Linley and E. Blease (who are at present acting as drill-sergeants), and Privates Richard Shaw (again ready for foreign service), Arthur Haddon (for home service) and Cecil Acton. Private John Thomason is at Sheffield in hos- pital; Private J. W. Jones at Camberwell. Lon- don; Private George Oldham is still in France (condition serious); Private- William Jackson in Lord Derby's War. Hospital, Winwiek ; and Private Jim Taylor at Lincoln. Lance-Corporal :Blease visited home last week-end. Corporal -Fred Kay and Private Walter Iladden .are, of course, back at the Front after being wounded. Private Stanley Gibbs- hopes shortly to come home from Toxteth- Park, Liverpool. Go On Duty Two Together.

To his mother thc,. Private says: "1 am writing these few lines while I am waiting to .go on duty to-night from ten to twelve. We do two hours on at once—two of us together. We are having it very quiet up to the present. They sent a few shells over us the other day, :end . . lam sorry to say a piece caught one of our men and killed him. Poor chap ! He was the first we have lost since coming here. I think someone from tymm will be coming on leave before long. It is better for those on leave now.; they have six days at home now. Don't get down- hearted and think we shall never come; we shall all have a leave, but it takes a long time to come round."

===== 0002601_19150828_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JOHN ST ITH.III.

A'olunteer Who Rejoined, AVoitntiNi At Dardanelles. - The news that Private John Statham, of the 6th South Lancashires, had been wounded at the Dardanelles was first received by Mrs. Joseph

Daniels, at The Rookery, in a letter from her own wounded husband. He said that "Jack had been slightly wounded, the same time as he was. They left Jack behind, as the troopship would .not hold all."

Private Statham himself has not written any- thing about his misfortune. Aged 37, Private Statham is a Lymm resident of thirty years' standing—although born at Sal- ford. As a lad he attended the village school; and now is employed in peace times as a labourer by Mr. John H. Holt, builder, Booth's Hill. He married nine years ago, and has a little boy and girl. The Lancashires .he joined in November—for he was on the National Reserve, having previ- ously served 13 years, with the Volunteers. He left for the pardanelles in early April. .

===== 0002601_19150828_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
STORIES FROM THE TRENCHES.

TERI:IIE2S' " lIVIPORTANT " JOB.

Seygi. Wifiocks Speaks Of An " Exciting Time."

• - Sergeant E. J. Widocks, formerly tram driver in Warrington, and now of the machine gun section of the Territorials, writing to Councillor Henshall, says :

"Dear Sir,—Just a few lines to say I have quite recovered from the shock of seeing dear old England. Glad to say I aril quite 0.K., and sincerely hope that both you and Mrs. Henshall are well. I was very pleased to have been able to see- you whilst on leave, although it was a very narrow squeak, as • I made three unsuc- .cessful attempts before managing to catch you. I think I'saw nearly all the good old friends— if I missed, any I apologise. "I have not much -to write about this time, as things .with us are rather on the quiet side. You •see the Battalion are not at present doing trench duty, but are on very important work— too important to tell you: I and the rest of the machine gunners have been doing trench work with another Battalion, and had rather an ex- 'citing time. Our 'friends' presented us on the afternoon of Monday last with a good supply of metal, which would have done a metal broker's heart good, could he have picked all the fragments up. One of their 'souvenirs' dropped clean into . . . but happily no one was injured, as I had previously withdrawn the boys on watch. The Brigade Chaplain also hap- pened to be in the trench, paYing us-a visit, and a shell dropped very near to him—he also escaped quite unhurt, and treated the matter quite lightly, although the shells were quite heavy. We are now back again with the Bat- talion—our own Battalion—and you know 'there's no place like home:" It is so much nicer to be with our own, although we were .with a very good regiment—and one that has made a name out here.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
Saw Too Many Young Men In Warrington.

"The thing, sir, that I was disappointed in on coming to Warrington was this. I strolled up 'Bridge-street to Market Gate, and was aston- ished to see 0, large number of young men— good, eligible, well-built 'young men—standing 'about. That made me think somewhat, for, honestly, limagined that young men in the good old town would .be scarce. I well 'know that all cannot join the Army, but I feel sure there are very many that can, and if they could only see what we See they would have no hesitation in joining us. The more men that join the sooner we shall be amongst you again. This is not written in a selfish spirit at all, but in a kind of • sorrowing mood. It is a great pity these young men seem to have enough spirit to be openly pointed at, and yet they don't seem to possess the spirit to join. I will not say more about it, but I dO -extend to them a hearty in- vitation to pay us a visit—in the King's uniform, of course.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
Beautiful Warrington Bridge.

"I must say a word of praise of your beautiful bridge. It is a magnificent structure, and re- flects the greatest credit on all those who had anything at all to do with it. It did seem large —in fact, • too large—for a town the size of War- rington, the first time or two I saw it, but that fault soon vanished the more I saw of it. I was asked repeatedly after coming back, 'What about the Bridge?' and I could only answer, 'The finest in. England.' I think in all Other respects to the two I have mentioned, that the old town had not changed very much. You now possess a V.C. hero, and I saw by the papers that you gave him a royal reception—and he well deserved it. My earnest hope is that you have to give. one of the 4th just such another "I think this must be all, or else I shall be try- ing the patience of the Censor, so with the best of wishes and luck, I will close, and remain, "Sincerely yours,

`-`' E. J. WIDOCKS.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
"JOE BUTLER."

Body Found And Buried By Warrington Men.

Rifleman Joseph Martin, of the Rifle Brigade, writing from the Front under date August 23rd to his mother at 77, Dudley-street, says : " We found a man the other day from War- rington. On his identity disc was his name— Joe Butler, and he belonged to the Bth Battalion Rifle Brigade. We gave him a decent burial, so if you can get to know who he is, it will perhaps ease the minds of his relations to know that he was buried all right, for it ..would per- haps be better for them to know his fate than to think he is missing. Some Narrow Shaves.

"Well, we haven't moved from the position from where we made the attack yet, I shall be glad when we do,- for it is a bit too warm for me. But never Mind, I.keep on-dodging it, and I shall get through all right. You see if I don't! I keep on having some narrow shaves, but narrow shaves are better than hard lines, aren't they? I was out on patrol the other night, abOut half-way between our trenches and the Germans, and the bullets were dropping all round me, but they didn't 'manage to get me. They can't, shoot well enough to hit me. Well, we are going down for a rest in a few days' time, and all the lads who came out with the battalion are getting furloughs, so it should not be long .before I get my turn.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
"The End Must Come."

"I don% know what you at home think of this war, but the 'sights I have seen, and the experiences I have had along this part of the line have made me and all the lads *Alt me in this 'mob.' pray for • the end. I have had my hands full, but never mind, we must keep on hoping for the best. The end must come, and Nature will not let it last much longer. Well, the shelling has eased up a bit now, so I will go and try to get a bit of sleep." Rifleman 'Martin enlisted in September, hav- ing previously been employed by Messrs. Pear- son. and Knowles as a boilermaker. He is twenty-two years of age. /

===== 0002601_19150828_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
MORE MEN FOR THE ARMY.

Lord Selhorne's Forecast.

1101%. FARMERS WILL BE AFFECTED.

An important speech on the position of agri- culture in the coming year was on Thursdaf made by Lord Selborne, Minister for Agricul- ture; at it ineeting in the -House of Lords tittended by representative, farmers from all parts of the country. The Central and Asso- ciated Chambers of Agriculture, the Royal Agricultural- Society; the :.Farmers' Club, and the Land Agents' Society were among the many societies represented.

One of the chief ,points in the speech was a warning that the calls of the Army upon agri- cultural workers will be greatly increased in the coming year, and an appeal to farmers to make fuller use of women and unskilled volun- teer labour on the land. In this connection Lord Selborne employed these significant words :

"What I forecast is going to happen in this next farming year is this—that men will be, taken and have to go from those districts and farms from which they have not hitherto gone." He announced ,that Lord Miler's Committve on Wheat Cultivation had. recommended giving the farmer a 455. a quarter guarantee on wheat, to commence with the next harvest, and gave the reasons why the Government Could not adopt it. The wheat acreage to-day is 30 per cent. more than it was in 1913, and there • are big increases in cattle and sheep stocks, and in view of the superabundant harvest in Canada and Australia, the labour difficulties due to the call of the colours, and the great financial stringency which wduld • follow the war, the Government could not incur the financial lia- bility which would be involved.

PRIVATE _JOSEPH DANIELS.

..AL Alexandria—Shot Through Willa

Hand.

Private Joseph Daniels, of '6, The Eookery, Booth's Hill—who, • as we reported last Week, has been wounded at the Dardanelles whilst serving with the 6th South Laneashires—now Writes to his wife. from Glymenopoulo, Alex- andria, to say his wound is a Oirapnel wound through the right hand, and is going on well.

/They think it's a walk-over here," he adds, "because we keep On going ahead. But where our diiision is we have to keep on going. A pal of mine is in the bed beside me writing this letter for me—as I can't use my right hand. He does not think he wil be in long, though, so I don't know how I . shall go oil for letter- writing after he has gone." Yesterday morning a field card repeated that Private Daniels continued to make good pro- gress..

- Private Daniels, who is a tanner with the Grappenhall Tanning Company, is married and has three children.

When a man talks well he is apt to talk too much.
