OUGHTRINGTON.

HORTICULTURAL SOCIETY.

Successful Annual Show Held At Broomedge.

conspicuous success attended the eleventh annual show held by the Broomedge and Dis- trict Horticultural Society at Broomedge •on Saturday from two to nine. Not only did the weather leave nothing to -be desired, but the interest was widespread and great—indeed the Hon. Secretary (Mr. E. Landless) stated that it had been found difficult to award the prizes for the best garden "so great was the level, of excellence." .

A schedule of extensive scope showed that 86 ,competitions were held, an average of two prizes, being given for each. The Prizes were gracefully presented by • Mrs. G. S. Tudor- Evans • (wife of the Rector of Oughtrington). MT. S. Davies (a vice-president) afterwards proposed hearty thanks .to Mrs. Tudor-Evans, .and expressed' the hope that many of those present would bring their flowers for the decoration of Oughtrington Church when they held their harvest festival. '(Hear, hear.) Mr. Clarke Marsland (of the committee) hav- ing seconded; the motion was heartily carried.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
Rector and the War.

The Rector—who acknowledged the vote on behalf of his wife—spoke of the pleasure they had both beei . afforded by being present and went on to say that this was the first Broom- edge Show he had had . an' opportunity Of at- tending. • The show, he thought, filled a very useful- purpose in the. neighbourhood. It was a great pity there were not shows throughout the county,. because in war-time people needed food .more than ever. -This shoy,- was Worthy .of every encouragement, and he would dO all he could • in the future to further,. the success of the society. (Applause.) An Efficient Committee.

On the motion of Mr. W., Wood, cordial thanks were also accorded to the judges— Messrs. G. Bennet of Lymm) and W. Harrison (Greenfield, Thelvirall).

The -following is the committee who man- aged the show so well: Messrs. P. Hindley (chairman), John Brazerntale, Joseph Brazen- P. Blease; S. W. Broadey,- R. Chorlton; E. Copeland, R. Gillibrand, A. Mclntyre, :W. 'Mc- Intyre, C. Marsland P. J. Peters, J. Price, H. Simpson, Joseph Spruce, J. Travis, W: Wardell, A. Webb, H. Vhitelegg,- E. Whitlow, William Wood, John Thomason (hon. treasurer), and E. Landless (lion. secretary).

Fighting In France.—.-Mrs. Percival, of Canal Bank, Grappenhtill, received word on Stinday. 22nd August, from ...her hush:And,' Frederick Percival, .now serving .with thc 2nd.,Cheshires in France, that the ...fighting had liven'. very severe, lie had just left the -trenches after, five days in them. _ Latchford Residents' Complaint.—At .Tues- day's meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council, a letter was read from four residents in Fairfield-road, Latchford, protesting against the erection of a motor garage at the rear of Fairfield-road on the ground that it 'interfered with a right of way and was in an undesirable position. Children were likely to run great risk from motor cars'. There was also risk of fire from the _storage of petrol, &C., in a wooden building.

GA:'LAN. 9

51, 53 el 557 BOIDEL E7:IIREET, WARELINGTOiI.

THE SOLDIERS' HOME.

-Fine 3c)iii-3freet Wo,rk Helped By Ail Setts.

" PE;'"vFNTiON."

Not the least. part' in the work now being performed by churches all over the country is the institution and upkeep of " homes " for the free use of soldiers in training. Doubtless there'are many chUrch movements from which "Tommy "—who stands not on , ceremony— would shrink in dread, owing to the narrow- mindedness of some religious people in the matter of behaviour; but to the eternal credit. of the churches it can be said that this time they have risen to the occasion in style little lacking. And " Tommy " appreciates it. Of this there is a forcible example in our own ' midst—the Bold-street Soldiers' Home, which is open every day from 2 to 9 p.m., and on Sundays from 7.45 to 9. This home was started on July sth by the Wesleyans, to whom the premises belong, but the co-operation and representation was invited, of all classes and sects, and the Wesleyans are connected with the institution only like anybody else. The work will be continued, support forthcoming, until the end of the war.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
A HAPPY SCENE.

When "The. Examiner" representative dropped in the home on Wednesday evening he was struck by the happy atmosphere which everywhere pervaded. In the middle of the spacious central room was a large table loaded with newspapers and magazines, and surrounded by forms filled with " Tommies." Not all, how- ever, were reading. Some were talking, whilst others appeared absorbed in Puzzle games and games of skill. At times one or two would go out for a stroll; and then more would stroll in and take their places at the magazine table or the billiard tables. Some would walk across to the refreshment stall and purchase for the modest sum of one penny a cup of coffee (or tea) and a cake; others would take mineral waters. At the same time the notes of the piano sounded on a popular air, and the men, with a laugh, looked up from their reading and their games and joined in the chorus heartilv—all except a pair who were far too en- grossed at the draught table, a magnificent type of neg To-soldier and one of the stewards. Several were standing round and watching the game with breathless interest.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW "GINGER" WAS "WIPED OUT."

On the semi-billiard tables and bagatelle boards play waxed keen amongst the younger men. • "Your turn, 'Ginger' !" one lad exclaimed. " Ginger " 'turned quickly to take his shot—and a good shot it was, too, considering that he had only learnt to play the week •before from one of the stewards. "Ginger" told us (after win- ning the game) how he himself had been "-wiped out" the previous afternoon by a soldier from the Infirmary who could only use one arm! He came from Orford Barracks. And there are many "Gingers" and many similarly-wounded men :who frequent the home—regularly fifty present, and .about ,eighty if everybody stayed the whole afternoon or .night instead of half an hour. Without announcement or ceremony a young lady stepped upon the platform and gave a melodious song, which earned her hearty ap- planse. It appears that. a thirty-minute concert is given every evening by different local artistes, young and old. Only a few nights before a first-class conjurer came down from Penk.eth, whilst su,ch well-known entertainers as Madame Sara Silcock, Madame B. Eckersley,• Mr. W. Whittle and Mr. Pearce. the baritone, had given their services. Recitals were also proving a popular feature. WHERE SILENCE REIGNS.

We walked into the writing-room without speaking, carefully shutting behind us the door. The silence was in striking comparison with the larger room. At one of the tables three soldiers wore penning a line "borne." For this purpose all sorts of materials had been provided, in- cluding suitably-headed notepaper, envelopes and postcards. Not far away was a steward who, if need be (and need does be) would assist " Tommy " in putting together his letter, even writing it for him! Something like twenty-five hundred sheets of ,notepaper and envelopes have been used in the writing-room during the last seven weeks. Of course this is not surprising, as the room is a most seductive resOrt—liand- somely furnisqied with lounge chairs; quiet and Cosy; and containing even a few books, which can be used for the asking. This next week there will be another room to match it; for the Conservative Club are sending along i full-sized hilliaxd table 'whieji will usurp the position of the reading-table in the large room; and so a reading room is to be introduced.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT THE TREASURER SAYS.

"We want to make the scope of the home as wide as possible," the treasurer remarked, when, conversing with "The Examiner "-man. "For this purpose we invite the assistance of all classes,, both in regard to finance and work , generally. Anyone who can come in of an evening and sing, recite, play, -will be gladly welcomed. We already have several bands of splendid ladies who do all the waiting at the refreshment stall—each band one day per week. There are great possibilities in the home. It now costs about £2 a week; the refreshments (which are the only things the soldiers have to pay for) practically pay for themselves; .for our loss on them is only very slight—but of course on Sunday nights we give them their refresh- ments. Quite a lot of soldiers, by the way, drop in during the day lust, for a rest and a cu P of tea or coffee, and then go out again. "The winter is coming on, however, and with the extra heating and lighting the cost will go up to £5 per week. Stilt, we are quite hope- ful that the money will be forthcoming—for we have never had to ask anybody twice; and our supporters, who include ChurchpeOple and all denominations, appreciate the home most highly. Many of them have paid us a visit and °express themselves as delighted. Again we have the sup- port of many firms; Messrs. Fletcher, Russell, for instance, have lent us -the cooking stove, and the Co-operative Society contribute each week five shillingsworth of confectionery:

"SOLDIERS MOST APPRECIATIVE."

"So we look forward to doing a still greater amount of good. The soldiers themselves are most appreciative of everything that has been done so far, but it will want keeping up con- stantly. We shall require a ,considerable amount of help, as you can see, and the invita- tion is therefore urgent: we must keep it interesting. '

"The home is open to all soldiers in the town —the wounded from the Infirmary, Whitecross, Lord Derby (Winwick) and other hospitals, from the Barracks, and any more who may be in the town. All these places we find repre- sented—although the men from the Winwick Hospital, who were allowed to come down to town every afternoon, have for some reason been recently stopped.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
A YEAR AFTER.

Sir,—More than a year has now passed since that August night when the King's Proclama- tion was read to his eager and anxious subjects, and the country is still as resolute and earnest in prosecuting the war to a successful con- clusion. An eye-witness of that fateful night thus describes the scene in London :

. I stood in the throng before Buckingham Palace when the King's Proclamation was read to the people. I moved from point to point in the crowd, listened, watched. Only a witness of the scene could understand the British heart that night. There were mothers and wives and daughters and sisters in the press to whom the portentous words were sentences of desolation. Not a tear pearled the cheek of a child or a grey-head. Instantly there were born a thousand new soldiers, who there received summons to the most desperate conflict upon which the Empire would ever embark. Their vision ref t the veils of the horizon, and disclosed the ominous German millions, and there was at least one gun in those silent ranks for each of their hearts. And they must have remembered the women they were to marry, and their mind must have caressed the wives they would leave behind, and they must have thought of the families dependent upon their eaznings and protection. But what they spoke to their souls only God heard. When their Majesties appeared all heads were uncovered. "God Save the King" sobbed through the night as though cathedral arches spread above us, 'and the notes were those of an anthem. The same writer, an American, describing London the next day, continues As was London that night, so is London this morning. The aspect of the city is unaltered. Save for the Press bulletins, and the cries of the paper-sellers, one meets with no sign of perturbation or excitement. Regiments marched through the streets at- tended by no greater crowds than one usually finds in the wake of parading soldiers. The unaltered price tickets in the shop windows proclaim the absence of business panic- The Strand maintains its accustomed appearance and seeks to lend assuranCeto the movement by calmly proceeding with its scheduled repairs. It seems to say to the passer-by: "Steady my friend. Why worry needlessly? Time alone controls affairs. All things are episodes to my vast experi- ence. For a thousand years I have borne the tread of regiments, and always the To- morrows of London are greater than her Yesterday. Take example from me, and attend to your allotted tasks, as I now pro- ceed with mine !"

At the present time, though prices have gone up a little, though we grieve a little when we see our wounded soldiers return, or see in the increase of sable trappings worn by the people our heart has not failed still to lace the for- midable task before us; not because we do not value the lives 'already' gone, but because we realise the necessity to the world at large that victory must be won at whatever cost. If our Army was small at the beginning our Navy was efficient and active and has, in addition to Protecting the coasts and ports of ourself and Allies, swept the enemy from the seas except the piratical submarine and the treacherous Zeppelin. What might have happened if the British Fleet had been less efficient or had been neutral spectators of German depredations it is fearful to contemplate, But for the active participa- tion of the British Fleet German kultur would now reign supreme from the North Sea to the Ural Mountains, and from the Arctic Ocean to the Cape of Good Hope. Liberty and freedom would have become as extinct as the dodo. Britain and the United States, if still in- dependent, would be compelled to do the behests of the German Autocrat. Ctesarism would reign from Pole to Pole and midnight, darker than the Middle Ages, would suffuse the world at large. All these miseries we have escaped from must be placed to the credit of the British Fleet; and no one knows better and recognises the fact more than Britain's Allies. It is this that stimulates the nation and its largely in- creased Army to go forward in this second year of strife with courage and hope of final victory. with full confidence in the motto of its ancient escutcheon "Dieu et mon droit." It would be far better that the whole world should die than to live under such a system of tyranny and debauchery as that outlined in German frightfulness and kultur. We do nat proclaim from the house-tops that God is with us, but we feel perfectly assured that Divine Provi- dence will not abandon its own creation, nor permit the ingenious power of evil to exter- minate those who truly place their reliance in its Power.

PHIL. SQUOD.

SHIPWRECKED.

Bridge-Foreman's Exciting Experiences Of Submarine.

ADRIFT ON SEA.

Mr. Harry Paine, contractors' foreman to Messrs. Alfred Thorne and Sons, Westminster, and general manager during the building of the new Warrington Bridge—who during that period resided in Ellesmere-road; Stockton Heath—visited Warrington last week after some unusual (and expensive) 'experiences he has had at sea. Mr. Paine sailed- from Glasgow, bound for Spain. There were also On board two other passengers, the 'crew of" eighteen, and a large general - cargo of "crush- ing" plant and machinery 'which was to be used in the work of. which Mr. .Paine was7to take charge at Huelva. All went well on the voyage until a German sub- marine was sighted only two hundred yards off. Without hesitation the crew of this enemy vessel fired a shot. across the bows to •let the Passengers know, it must stop; and then hoisted the flag as a signal that the ship must be abandoned.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
LOST ALL BUT PASSPORT.

Things were done briskly on board. Two boats were lowered and manned without waste of time, the engineers being called up from the engine rooms just as they were—with hardly any. clothes on. Biscuits, tinned foods and water casks were hurriedly thrown into the boats, and in a very short time one was sailing away, quickly to .be followed by the other— which had to wait for the captain, who was the last to leave. In this second. ship Mr. Paine . was. None of the cargo or luggage could be taken—it had all to be abandoned, and out :of nearly fifty pounds' worth of personal. luggage Mr. Paine came 'away with only his, passports, which had been signed by Sir Edward grey in London a few daYs. before. The loss to .the firm he represented must be correspondingly great.

IN " TITANIC " LIFEBOAT!

On the departure of the crew the first boat had been well in front, and both were making as fast as possible for the shore. . On board the ship they had not noticed the rough state of the sea; but now, floating only. in open boats, they were appalled by it. None were .afraid, and Mr. Paine never felt any effects—for he was well clad, and at regular intervals smoked a cigar and ate a biscuit. But amongst the crew seasickness was acute, and they were utterly helpless. The mind was carried back three years to when, •in this identical - boat, the :passengers of the ill-fated ". Titanic " had floated in the ice-cold waters of the Atlantic thousands of miles away. And now they, were to a great extent helpless. Yet strange to say they reached the lighthouse in twelve hours, whilst the first boat did not come in until two houts later. A most striking fact was that during the whole of those twelve arduous hours—from morning to sundown—not the slaightest hint of thirst was giveri, although there was a good supply of water on board.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
REACHING THE LIGHTHOUSE.

Arrived, at the lighthouse, -the shipwrecked voyagers were helped in and narrated their ex- periences. At once they got in communication with the coast, and a tug was despatched from shore. Meanwhile a 'look out was kept for the first boat, and on its arrival the lighthouse men did all in their power for the unfortunates. After three hours the tug was sighted, and two and a half hours later the whole party of 21 arrived safely on the mainland. Here they were ..met by the Shipwrecked Mariners' Society, who provided those who needed them with clothes, and all with food. The kindness also of the natives was most notable-7--and should be strongly, emphasised. From Glasgow Mr. Paine .proceeded to War- rington, and stayed with friends at 81, Man- chester-road. He started out again for Spain on Monday this week_ "And," said' out interviewee in conclusion, "I don't see what good it can be doing them to sink these vessels, for it certainly is costing them more than the ships are worth to do it!"

===== 0002601_19150828_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
AT FAIRBOURNE FOR MAYOR.

Mr. Harry Paine's acquaintance with War- rington,- dates back to August, 1911, when he laine over from Westminster to work as con- tractors foreman for Messrs. Alfred Thorne and Nms On the new bridge. Since the completion of that fine piece of work he has been living at Pairbourne--engaged on the -sewage develop- raent of the estate of the Mayor (Alderman Peacock). Indeed, •he would have remained there had he not ' previously promised to superintend this particular " job " in Spain.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 28, 1915.

IN THE COURTS.

ETHICS OF BETTING.

Magistrate's Complaint 01 Wasted

Time.

"It is abominable that n a period 'like the present the time of four riagistrates and two lawyers should be wasted in trying a ( Oil- teraptible case- like this," was the remark/ of Councillor . Henry Roberts, Chairman of the Bench, at the 'Warrington Borough Court on Tuesday, regarding a case of assault. The parties were John Ashton, wiredrawer, 18, Egerton-street, Howley, who, was charged with having, on the Bth. inst., assaulted Thomas Rigby, file-cutter, 141, Ellesmere-street. —Mn. A. Browne appeared for the complainant, and Mr. T. S. Steel defended. Mr. Browne said the assault had arisen out of a very simple matter, namely; as to which of two men had worked the longer at Messrs, Rylands' Bros. Ltd.defendant or a friend. The complainant and defendant met in Howley- lane on the night in question and began to dis- cuss the bet with .the former that a man whose name ,he mentioned had not worked longer for Messrs. Rylands than he (defendant) had, ".if he could be given two years." The bet was accepted, and eaeh man .deposited half-a-crown with .a Mr, Robert Eyes, and immediately all set off to a friend's, house, and he was asked about the length of his service. He replied that; strange .to say, only a few week ago he had come across his first wage sheet, showing that he started at Messrs. Rylands in May, 1860, where- upon the stakeholder told defendant he had "lost by a long way," and the sum of ss. was handed to the complainant. The complainant and defendant were then left alone, and the latter threatened that unless the -2s. 6d. was .returned to him by Rigby he. would " bash " him, and.. struck him in the mouth. The tom- plainant was knocked into the 'doorway of the hOnse, and when he got up the defendant Struck him in the left ear and knocked him in the gutter. His watch, a new suit of clothes and false teeth,were damaged. . Two women who sa* the assault picked .the complainant out of the mud. Rain Was falling heavily at the time.

Mr. Steel was ,cross-examining witness as to the nature of the bet, when Mr. Roberts inter- vened and said: "We are not here to discuss the ethics of betting. If these men are fools enough to enter into a bet on such a con- temptible and paltry thing it does not affect the question of the assault. It is .tt, waste of time for four magistrates and two lawyers to try a contemptible case like this." ,Mr. Steel: The cross-examination has a bear= ing on the question of provocation for the assault. Mr. Rbberts: I don't cam .what the 'provoca- tion was, even admitting it, but it is disgust- ing to both of them to bet under such circum- stances. The only question to decide was whether there had been an .assault. Mr. Browne: Why. did not the defendant apologise when we wrote to him? Mr. Steel: Because we have not assaulted him.

In further cross-examination by Mr. Steel, complainant denied that .he taunted the de- fendant with the remark "That is the way we take puppies down." Two witnesses gave evidence in corroboration of the assault. • Alleged Bet on a Certainty. For the defence Mr. Steel said that if his ca§e was correct and the complainant had admitted he was betting on a certainty, it was a mean and contemptible thing on the part of Rigby. Mr. Roberts: That may be, but we are not try- ing that. Mr. Steel: They could quite understand that the defendant -would be annoyed when he had been done out of his half-crown. The defend- ant admitted calling Rigby a did not strike him. Mr. Roberts: Two witnesses have testified that he did assault Rigby. Mr. Steel: I quite agree, but I have no evidence of what actually took place. Ile in- tended to call Councillor G. Archer, who would Prove the provocation. The, Chairman: What we think is that there has been an assault,, and I do not see how you are going to disprove it. You have had an opportunity of cross-examining the witnesses on the point. Mr. Steel: After that expression I will not call the defendant as witness. A fine of 20s. was imposed, and an advocate's fee of tl is. allowed.

Three Months For Writer Of Begginu

The Case Outlined.

Wilderspool-road,- and sent in a begging 'tette-r. He was invited in, and. the Rev. Waller, after a short conversation, asked his visitor if he could wait twenty minutes and, receiving kreply. in the affirmative, called on w neighbouring ministerial \ brother—the Vicar of Stockton Heath—obtained his advice and sent for the police. He took the .man in charge, and escorted him to the Police Station, just succeeding in pre- venting his prisoner from 'jumping into the river. On the following morning prisoner pleaded guilty to a charge of begging, ,and was remanded for a week; pending the institution inuiries by the police as to bis identity.

Didn't Understand The Charge; Charged on Thursday with obtaining charit- able contributions by fraud, Edwards pleaded not guilty. • The Chief Constable pointod out to the Bench that a week ago the prisoner pleaded guilty to a similar charge anti he (Mr.. Nicholls) thought he didn't quite understand the present charge. Do you admit handing in a begging letter?" the Chief Constable asked the prisoner. "Yes," Edwards replied. "Well, then, you plead guilty." And the Chief Constable went on to outline the history of the case.

Waller, was read by Mr. Nicholls : " Honoured sir,—l hope you will indeed for- give this liberty, as I feel it very difficult to express how very deeply I regret the necessity which: compels me to write this, but I am in great difficulties. It grieves me very much to have to take these steps. sir, but I am so hard ' hit- by this war. Trusting you will .indeed forgive this liberty, and take a kindly 'view of my awful 'position.—Believe me to be,, sir, yours obedi- ently. ALEXANDER EDWARDS." Prisoner .paid he _ thought the Rev. Waller would have assisted him with a Pail- of shoes. When he offered him money he refused it and said that he could not really repay it. . The Prisoner's Record.

The Chief Constable said that it would ap- pear that 'prisoner had been going about the country carrying on the swindle. He had a six months' conviction against him at Ports- mouth. He (the Chief Constable) had a number of reports about pri- soner, but no good purpose would be served by reading them. Even the. prisoner's own father ,spoke of hhu in shocking terms. , Prisoner: Yes, because I was turned out quite young on the streets of London, without a penny in my pocket, to get my living as best I could. So, if I haven't had a hard life, who has? When I try to get honest employment one of

the London detectives writes and tell them what I have been charged with, and, of course, they don't want me then. - The Chief Constable: No, I should think not. Prisoner was sentenced to three months' im- prisonment. '

TREATING SOLDIERS.

Grave \Yarning To Erring Civilians.

The Chief Constable, at the Police Court on Tuesday, during the hearing of a charge of drunkenness against a woman named Mary Graham, said that the prisoner, along- with otner women, was in the habit of taking wounded soldiers to houses and obtaining drink for them and behaving in a disgraceful manner. He had been in communication With the Military Authorities and it was probable they would make it an offence for civilians to supply drink to the wounded soldiers. Motor cars from one. of the local military hospitals had been out in Warrington until three o'clock in • the , morning bringing in soldiers from houses where they have been taken by such women.

The prisoner was ,s_ent to gaol for one month

BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

MONDAY.

Before the Mayor (in the chair), Mr. S. Wright, Aldermen Pierpoint, ..Smethurst, Bolton and James Evans, Messts. W. Furness and T. Locker.

A plain clothes officer happened to be on the premises, and he ejected the 'prisoner, who continued his .bad behaviour in the street and was taken into cutitody.— Prisoner was fined 95., ,with the alternative of seven days' imprisonment.

THE REASON WHY.

Young \Willa!' Who kppropriatNl A Coat Because It Was Raining.

Susannah Davidson, a young woman, living at 5, St. Mary's-street, was remanded until Friday on a charge of stealing a plush coat value 205., the property of Lucy Clough. The Chief Constable stated .that prosecutrix went out on the afternoon in question, leaving the coat hanging in the lobby.. On returning she missed_ it, and the matter was reported to the Police. Inquiries were, - made, and ulti- mately .Mr. Dendy, cycle agent, Knutsford- road, came to the. Police Station, and said he had reason to believe that prisoner had the stolen coat. Detectives Anderson and Skelton saw the prisoner, Who said she knew Mrs.. Clough twelve months ago. As it was raining en the afternoon in question, she went to Mrs. Clough's and, getting no response to her knock at the front door, entered the house at the back, and took the coat. It might have been raining then, but Pris6ner had continued to wear the garment practically all ,the week. She had already served several years in a reformatory, and it was a question of what could be done for her. She had had no regular employment since she returned -home tlVo years ago. The Adjourned Hearing.

The Superintendent stated, When the case came up again yesterday morning, that the Police Court Missionary had had several inter- views with the prisoner, and was desirous that the case should be adjourned till the 31st December. He had told her that if she went back to her work and behaved properly, she would hear nothing more of the matter.

Mr. Williamson Went on to say that prisoner went into a reformatory in 1909, .at the age of fourteen, for stealing. She came out of the reformatory two years ago, and since then had never settled down. The longest period she had kept a situation for, during that time, was three months, when she was employed as. a bar- maid. There was now no reason why she should not settle down to habits of industry. She got out of control of her mother at the age of fourteen. The case was adjourned till December 31st.

RAU.WAI-FRESPASSING.

Warrington Men ‘ThoLWalked Along

Line To Get Drink.

Three men, George Andrews, of Cartwright- street; 'Arthur Wilkinson, of 16, Hepherd-street, and Wm. Clarke, of 8, Fountain-street, were summoned at the Borough Court yesterday for trespassing on the L. and N.-W. near Lytton's Mill, on August 11th.

Railway lietective-Sergeant Hewitt said that at 10.40 on the day in question, he, in company with a colleague, saw Wilkinson come along by the metals and go through the gate of the cross- ing. Asked what he was trespassing for, he said he was going for some cigarettes. Another of the defendants Caine along afterwards and threatened the signalman, who had closed the gates, telling him what he would do if he didn't open them. Then the third came along and got over the gate. The -then had been a good deal of trouble in that way. They were in the habit of leaving their work and walking along the line to get to a public-house to obtain drink. The defendants were let off with a fine of ss, each, the Chairman of the Bench remarking that•tbey were being leniently treated, but he wanted it to be a warning to other 'people.

PERSISTENT CRUELTY.

Wife Who Had Eight Black Eyes In Three Years. Alfred Edward Cox, of 24, Napier-street, was summoned by his wife, Mary Ellen Cox, at the Warrington Police Court yesterday morning, for persistent cruelty. Mr. Arthur Browne appeared for the complainant. Threatened WRIT a Razor.• - Mr. Browne stated that defendant was a joiner earning 395. a week and, at present, an additional 10s. a week overtime. The couple were married in April, 1912. Since then com- plainant had had no less than eight black eyes. On one occasion defendant gave her two black eyes. He had threatened her with a razor and taken a hammer to her. The climax came on Saturday night, when defendant came in, under the influence 'of drink, at ten minutes past ten, and began to quarrel with her because his supper was not right. He said he had had five gills at ,the Mersey Vaults. Complainant called him a drunken hog, whereat he struck. her in the eye, cutting it badly. She told him she would apply for a separation order, and he said that. if she did not get. out that. night- ho' would push her out. Complainant was, afraid. to live with her husband. He told her to go and get a separation order when she threatened to do 80, Wing that he would be "glad to be " of her. .

Not Satisfied With Wages. Defendant denied that he said he would turn his wife out, and alleged that the cause of the trouble was that his wife was not satisfied with his. wages. He had never lost a day's Work since they had. been married. He had given her an- average of 30s. a week, and she said it (lid not keep them. On Saturday night she came- to the Mersey Vaults when he was in. there, and asked for "the other shilling," as. he had only given he 295. that morning. He told her .she would get no shilling from him, and she went round to her sister's place. That was part of the cause of .the trouble. She was in the habit of going round there much too often. She thought more of .her sister's place than of her .own home. When he went round there he wag abused in all sorts of ways. He did not deny that lie had struck her, but he thought he, at least, had had a good deal of provocation. He had to prepare meals for himself on Sunday. and Monday. His dinner on Monday consisted of bread and butter, and that was the kind of thing he had had to put up with. . Asked if he had any objection to a. separa- tion order being f granted, defendant replied in the negative.. In fact,. he said,. he thought it

was th-e best solutioii' of the Matter. lie had had enough. An ordel...fot the payment by defendant of 12s, a week th his wife was made out.

Settled Out of tourt. The case before the Court was about the ownership of a donkey, and both plaintiff and defendant were costermong,ers. They were conducting their own appeals, and the judge had passed au amusing M.orning with them. At noon he announced.:

"The Court will now adjourn for luncheon, and I trust that .when we all meet again. these two gentlemen will have settled their dispute out of Court?' -

When the sitting. Was resumed the plaintiff had a black eye, while the defendant's froirit teeth were missing, and his nose battered. "Well, yer honour,". said the defendant, when called on to speak, "the case 'as bin settled out n' Court. ..Tim's given me a rare good 'iding, and I've given lin the'donkey."

FREEDOM FOR POULTRY.

Whenever possible fowls should be allowed their -freedom, since under such COnditonS they are healthier and considerably cheaper to maintain. Fowls that are at liberty are -able, when they have access to any land save heavy clay or sand, to secure a large proportion of their own food, and there is no artificial food yet invented that is so good for poultry as grubs, worms, insects, 'seeds, &c. A flock of birds enjoying their freedom can be kept in good condition .upon half as much food as those confined in rUns, and we have often kept large numbers of fowls, both growing . stock and adults, upon a single meal a 'day. At a rough estimate we should say that 50 per cent. of the poultry in this country' are too fat, and of this 50 per cent. quite 45 :per cent. are those that are confined in runs. Birds at liberty are not nearly as likely to become too fat as are those having- comparatively little exercise. Not only, however, are poultry able to obtain a good pro- portion of their own living when at liberty, hut their manure is an exceedingly valuable asset, one that is almost invariably underestimated. The dung of fowls is exceedingly rich and con- centrated, and in a pure condition is worth to market • gardeners about £3 to £4 per ton.- IPoultryman7 üi "Farm and-Home."

TUE WORLD'S FOOD SUPPLY.

The Board of Agriculture and Fisheries have receivgd the following information from the International Agricultural Institute : . The. total ~Production •of crops in 1915 in the undermentioned countries is estimated as ' follows: Wheat in the United 'Kingdom, Italy, Spain, Switzerland, ~'Russia in Europe, India, Japan, Tunis,. United States and Canada (win- ter wheat only), 1,383,889,000 cwt., or 17.9 per 'cent. above last year's production. Barley in the same countries, except India and Canada, 414,411,000 cwt., or 19 per cent. labove last year's Production.. . Oats in the same countries as barley, except Japan,. 755,136,000 cwt., or 21.1 per cent. above last year's production. Rye in the same countries as for oats, except England, Scotland and Tunis, 514,365,000 cwt., or 18.7 per cent., above last year's production. The estimated production of maize in the United States is 1,458,993,000 cwt. and of potatoes 230,899,000ewt., or 9.2 and 6.2 per cent. respectively above last year's production.

FARMER APPOINTED, NIAGISTRATE WHILE ON ACTIVE SERVICE.

The . appointment . of • Lieutenant-Colonel George E. Sayce as one of the justices.. for the county of Lancaster., whilst on active service, is a striking and unique honour.. Colonel Sayee, who lives at Bank End Farm, Halebank, near Widnes, is one of the best- known farmers in South Lancashire. He was a Territorial officer in the Roye- Engineers when the war broke out, and immediately volunteered .for foreign service. Being drafted to Egypt in October, he is now in the Dar- danelles.

The new magistrate has for several years past been 'one of the leading 'members of the Liver- -1)001 and District' Farmers' Club. Colonel Sayce has also three sons with the colours.

FARid AZIB GARDEN. ~

MILK it ECORDS.

First Year's Work hi Cheshire shows

Great Variations.

The first annual report of the -Cheshire Milk Recording Society says that the herds of the members were divided into two Sections, and two recorders were appointed. The milk yields of 1,034 cows .and heifers have been recorded. The tabulated results show a wide variation in the yield of individual cows. The highest yield of a cow during the recording year was 17,7401 b., equal t'o 1,716 .gallons, for '350 days, while the- lowest for the same period was 3,1711 b., or 306 gallons for 308 days, heifers not included. The facts revealed by the records would enabie farmers to determine more readily which cows are profitable. to keep in the herd, and also which it is suitable to breed from. Several members made a start in the testing of the milk of their cows for quality, and it was expected that this would develop rapidly as the advan- tage, -from a business point of view, was more fully realised. Owing to the generous financial support of. county ladies and gentlemen, who had become honorary members, the expense of carrying on fhe .society had been to members comparatively small. It was confidently e:z- pected that there would be a large increase of members for the second year! • .The accounts show members' subscriptions. £6 10s.; donations, £66; levies from members' of is. per cow, £4B' 25.; grant from Board of Agri- culture £44 .55.; and other receipts, £l4 4s. 3d. After paying expenses there- is a balance in hand. of £2'. is. 101 d.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
CHESHIRE WHEAT.

Bah wheat and Oats are full average crops in Cheshire- so far as the yield of grain .is con- cerned, the plenteous rains having resulted in a full .condition- of the ears. The farmer now only.:-needs'.a continuance of fine weather to enable him to harvest these crops'. in good con- dition. Cheshire •is not a. heavy wheat-growing 'county, hut the bulk produced this year will be greater than in previous years. When the war and Other conditions last autumn sent up wheat prices and the cost of bread many farmers in Cheshire extended the acreage of wheat-growing on their farms.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD CHEESE PRICES.

The market for cheese this year has been the best in the history of the cheese- industry .of Cheshire owing ,to. the heavy buying .for the Army, and though for the moment prices have fallen compared with the sinter prices, when as much as 100 s. per hundredweight was paid for the finest Cheshire cheese, the Inarket still remains active, with best cheese finding a ready sale at 745. per hundredweight. The prospects of a heavy summer make of cheese are excellent. There is an abundance of grass everywhere, and a heavy yield of milk is assured. Farmers agree that they,have never seen so much keep, in the shape of grass, on the countryside.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
VILLAGE WAR FOOD' SOCIETIES.

The Board of Agriculture has issued a leaflet on the subject of Village War Food Societies, which contains suggestions for the formation and working of societies to take an interest in food production. There are scattered all over the country vacant building plots, uncultivated waste areas, and common lands, and the societies, it is proposed, might Make arrange- ments for cropping such land, and thus adding to our ,home resources. It is also suggested that they should concern themselves with the growing of vegetables and fruit, the keeping of pigs, poultry, pigeons, rabbits and bees, and references are given to a number of leaflets issued by the Board in which information on these subjects may be found. In so far as the societies 'aim at increasing the amount Of food prothiced,:at home and checking waste,' there •can be no doubt that they will be doing good work. The leaflet is numbered Special No. 32.

SAVE THE POTATO CROP.

In some eases the potato is in grave danger .of destruction from • disease, one& more show- ing: the risk •of omitting to spray. The mis- fortune .is that since the wet weather cern- meneed- suceessful spraying has been next to impossible, inasmuch as the rain is apt to wash off the solation of sulphate of copper and lime from the-leaves directly -or-soon''after-.it has. been &Posited. I examined a plantation. of r}otatoel without observing an unhealthy con- dition,,litnd within a couple of days it had ap- peared right through the crop. Here the owner decided to cut off the tops at -once, as the tubers: were sound and well developed, rather than to spray and leave them to fate, for the weather was wet every day. Should the oppor- LtuaitY arise I believe it would be a wise plan Ito spray vigorously a. clean plant if the weather is fine and dry, and thus to remove or minimise the rid:.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0204.txt | included=true | included =====
HEDGES AND DITCHES.

Once staying with • a N orfolk farmer in August I noticed that simultaneously with the completion of his harvest work 4n a very large farm, his hedges were trimmed for the winter. 'At this moment labour is not so plentiful, but this work is all-important, and can be accom- plished best by old men, who, if skilled, take more' care than their juniors. • Well-kept hedges and ditches contribute to 'clean farming, and there can be no possible freedom from insect and parasitic pests if they are, neglected. It is not that weeds—and a crowded hedgerow is the worst source of weeds on the farm—rob the soil of nutriment which is intended for, Culti- vated plants, .so much as the fact that they encourage all the enemies of the crops, which always injure them, and Sometimes destroy thein altogether. This work is best done by the 'piece, because it is done better and 'quicker by pieceworkers who understand it than by labourers who do not.—James Long, in "Farm nd Home."

===== 0002601_19150828_art0205.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

THE CELERY MAGcOT.

This ,destructive pest often-' makes its appear- ance during this ' and next month, and if not speedily checked soon ruins the plants, especially if they are allowed to become dry the roots. Broadcast sprinklings of soot and lime or wood • ashes in equal, quantities will often prevent the fly, attacking the plants, but should the maggot have established_ itself, _ then only

rigid .hand-picking will avail, as the maggot conceals itself between the tissues of the leaves and defies all insecticides. The plants should be very closely watched, and if any traces of the maggot are seen hand-picking-should at once be performed. Sometimes one picking suffices, but if a second attack is made then the picking must be repeated. '

===== 0002601_19150828_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
EARLY POTATOES.

A friend writing to us the other day told us that he had seldom Seen the disease so rampant as it is this year. This no doubt is in a great measure due to the heavy rains we have had, these encouraging it to spread very fast. Such being the case, it would be advisable to at once lift all potatoes that are nearly, ripe in order to prevent the disease germs reaching the tubers. In many districts early potatoes are quite a failure, this having been caused by the Afrost which we had in May. when the •stems were first coming through. Nothing is gained by leaving the tubus in the soil, and the best • . - way- will be to • clear the,. ground and fill the space • with. 'winter greens or cabbages, savoys. 13ruSsels‘ sprouts, S:c., which now that the soil is so moist and warm will soon get a hold.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
SHOWING VEGETABLES.

There is much to be .said favour of show- ing vegetables in their finest possible forM, .but as these are required for food their fitness for the table should always be taken into considera- tion. ['Take leeks, for example. No doubt, well blanched examples of moderate dimensions are excellent when well cooked, but the tendency of the present-day •exhibitor is to show us stems as large round as broom-handles, and with from 12in. to 14in. of blanched st,em. Such stems no doubt have a very imposing appearance on the exhibition table, and match Aell the celery. [ parsnips, onions, runner beans, carrots and similar products. The question May well be [ asked, of what value are such Monstrosities for the table? By the exhibitor well-blanched stems of the leek of the Size useful for cooking are 'considered weak and wanting in substance. —Wiek, in " Farm and Home.". • • •

PRESENTATION OF THE CUP.

Mr. T.

E. Garrett (vice-chairman of. the

—.— Association) asked Mr.'W. Peter Rylands (chair- man of the Conservative Association) to present the cup to the winners. Mr. Rylands handed the Cup to Mr. A. Broady (captain) and heartily congratulated the team. . They had certainly bowled splen- didly throughout the •- competition. He. had watched their triumphant career with interest, but he had always felt they met their masters when they met Rylands. Bros.—(laughter)—who were always accustomed to winning. It was rather a blow to them to lose. He had, how- ever, much pleasure in congratulating the Senior Conservatives as the best team in War- rington. (Applause.) M. Broady was pleased that •-e Conserva- tives had won • the cup a second time. All the games had been played in a gentlemanly .spirit, and his team had ,found- many friends. .If they did not. win the cup next year he hoped that Rylands'& might do so, for they had competed in a very Sportsmanlike manner. (Applause.) - Mr. J. Woods (captain of Messrs. Rylands's team.), who did- not play in the final, replied on behalf of his club, and said that the better team had won on the day's play. Rylands's had, he thought, been very unfortunate. They had, however; won the cup once, and he believed they stood a good chance of winning it next year. (Hear, hear.) Mr. john Wells (chairman of the Conserva- tive Bowling Green Committee), proposing a ,vote of thanks to Mr. Rylands for his services, said that Mr. Rylands, by his presence that day, Showed that he took an interest and a pleasure in the sport of the employes of his firm, and of the members of the Conservative Association.

Reddish seconded. He was sure the games had afforded Mr. Rylands pleasure in a double sense, for not only was- he greatly interested in the recreation scheme of Messrs. Rylands, but he must feel much pleasure in seeing them the runners-up to the Senior Conservatives. (Ap-

plause.) The resolution was carried by acclamation

THE NEED FOR RECREATION.

Mr. Rylands, replying, said it was a real Pleasure to watch the doings of Rylands's Recreation Club, whether it was in bowls, :cricket, football, billiards, or anything else. This was a time when ones thoughts went in other directions, and sometimes people con- tended that we ought not to enjoy ourselves at all. He was sure that was wrong. The war was always in . our minds and always giving us anxiety, but if we did nothing but think about it we were apt to get in a very bad frame of mind. We must get away from the contempla- tion of the War at times, and it Was only right that we should, in a quiet way, enjoy a certain amount of recreation. There was none more suit- able than a game or bowls. '(Applause.)

THAMES SWIMMING RECORD

A remarkable swim was accomplished on Tuesday in the Tham2s by Miss Eileen Lee, a nineteen-year-old daughter of the lock-keeper at Teddington. Starting frora the Tower Bridge at 8.41, she proceeded on the up-tide to Twick- enham, a distance of 18 miles, in shr. Bmin. 15sec. Turning on the tide, Miss Lee came back as, far as Kew Bridge. For the total distance of 212 miles her full time was. 6hr. 38min. This constitutes a record. Miss' Lee only left the water owing to her eyes being much. inflamed. She frequently encountered head winds and choppy water.

FOOTBALL.

COMING RUGBY SEASON.,

The Intermediate League.

Will all those who intend to enter the above League conimunicate with the League Secretary at once? The committee wish to make the season as successful as possible, and their efforts will be ably backed up-by the Wring- ton Club Committee, three members of which have been co-opted on the League Committee. A deputation from the League met the War- rington Committee during the week, and the following decisions were arrived at : A letter having been received from a promi- nent works -in town asking if they could- join the, League, the Warrington Committee recommend to the League Committee " That boys under 18 on April 30th, 1916, belonging to a works may be combined 'to forth a team, pro- viding they are not old boys . belonging to a schogl already in the League." It is proposed to play a schoolboys' match and an Intermediate League match every Saturday during the season at 'Wilde:spool.

The committee will try to arrange inier-townr matches for both Leagues. A well-known East Lancashire town team will be played, probably in October.

The ground at Wilderspool will be opened each evening for practice for the Intermediate League. Balls will be provided for practice on application to the groundsman. There will be a meeting at Wilderspool on Thursday next, Septembfr 2nd; at 7.30 p.m. Those who intend to become members of the League are invited to send a representative. Application for membership should be made as early as possible to Mr. E. Jackson Mee, hon. sec., Mersey View, Penketh; or Mr. A. Malcolm, hon. treasurer and registering sec., 113, Wilderspool.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 28, 1915.

THE CLERK AND THE PARSON.

Ecclesiastical Encouniers lin The Days Of Long Ago.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
A CHAPTER OF STORIES.

By ARTiIUR

The intelligent reader who has fornied his own mental picture of the Parish Clerk will expect yet another crop of stories. That such officials as the clerk and the person with func- tions that run so close together should exist side by side, and that things should always run smoothly, is perhaps beyond expectation in this world of frailties. The possibilites for friction will be the better realised when, we remember that the clergy in the bad old days were often pluralists, that is-, they held more livings than one, and as they could not pos- sibly give their presence to three or four par- ishes, the parish clerks must perforce perform the duties and in the process became the rulers of the church and its services. It is in the shameful state of things that resulted from absentee Clergymen that the degradation of church services in many wishes is ex- plained, and the blame must rest with the clergy and with the system rather than with the unlettered clerks to whom the manage- ment of affairs fell.

Let us again say that we set forth nothing in malice, and record only authenticated stories. Having laughed at them, let us blush for them and rejoice that they are things of the past.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
A SHAME TO DISTURB THE GOOSE.

In the parishes of Norfolk a century ago the rectors and vicars lived away from their work, and paid among them the salary of a curate to take as many services as he could. If the curate arrived there was a service, but not otherwise. A month or more would pass without his presence, and the clerk waited for his approach before ringing the bell. On one of those visits the curate found the clerk looked rather puzzled_ " I was wonderin', sir," he said, " would yew mind a-preachin' from the readin' desk to-day." "Yes, but you know the pulpit .is the proper place. Why not from the pulpit?" Then the truth had to come out. "Well, you see, sir, I have an old guse a-settin' on eggs in the pulpit. It was a good place because as how when the door's shut she can't wander. She'll be 'arf her eggs to-morrow an' twould be a shame to disturb her to-day." Most of the clergy of half a century ago could tell good stories of their clerks. Lady Verney, in her "Essays and Tales," sketched a Clerk who must have been common in the last century, who had been appointed sixty years before because he was the only man in the parish who could read, and who mentally placed himself upon a level with Her Majesty's judges in • his importance to the State. In vain the clergyman complained of his English. .Was he who -had read the lessons "afore that young jackanapes was born to be teached how 'twere to be done " ? It was the pastor who couldn't "spike English nayther."

• Another of similar ancient standing was giving his opinion to the vicar upon the course of events. " Ali! I've seen mony changes. I've'seen four vicars. First there was Canon D-, then there was Mr. T----, who's now a bishop, and then Mr. came, and now you've coom, an' we've wossened every toime." A late vicar of Alnwick used fo say that the 'old clerk there looked upon the church and parish as his own, and when he became reminiscent used to tell how many "kewrats" lie had trained in their duties. His only fault was in his reading and responses. He had learned them off and would not, or could not. unlearn. To the end of his long life he read "The Lord sitteth upon the meek."

===== 0002601_19150828_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
SEVERE ON THE BISHOP.

Not even a bishop could make an impres- sion or alter the ways of some clerks. On an occasion in a Lancashire parish the bishop had come to make an appeal for some special object. But it was the clerk's habit to take the collection before the sermon, 'and he prb- ceeded to do so on this occasion, although he had been told that the appeal must come first and the sermon afterwards. The vicar leaned forward and in 4, stage whisper told him to wait until the end of the sermon. The clerk slowly turned, gave a condescending look at the bishop, and said in- a tone that was heard through the church, "It won't make a ha'porth o' difference," and went on with the collection.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
JOHN WESLEY'S JOKE.

Few parsons have come out victorious when there has been conflict with the clerk: Among them we may perhaps place John Wesley, though the memory must have troubled him in his later and more zealous years. When serving at Epworth he had a con- ceited clerk, whose pride led him into all sorts of extravagances. He regularly begged the rejected gowns and clerical clothes of the clergymen and persuaded Wesley to give him one of his wigs.' The clerk's head was small, and when the wig was placed 'upon it, the effect appears to have tickled Wesley exceed:. ingly. The opportunity to thine the clerk's pride seemed too good to be missed, and when they were about to enter the church Wesley said, ‘f I shall choose my own psalm this morn- ing, John. I will give out the first line and you will repeat the next. one, as usual."

When the- time came for the psalm, Wesley gave out "Like to an owl in ivy bush," and the clerk responded, " That rueful thing am I." The congregation, with the vision of a head half buried in a wig, did their best not to laugh outright. A century ago the church at Liston wit- nessed an incident in which both parson and clerk appear in a curious light. It was usual at that period for the clergyman to exchange his surplice for a gown before entering the pulpit. To do this he retired into the vestry, a psalm being sung meanwhile. On this occa- sion the rector, Mr. Wallace, found that he had left his sermon at home. Rather than face the congregation without his manuscript he slipped out into the churchyard and made his escape.

The clerk meanwhile droned on with the psalm, lengthening it out most painfully and keeping one eye on the ves,try door. He added several doxologies and dragged them out at the same slow speed. Going at last into the vestry and realising the situation, he returned, angry, to pro- nounce the benediction.

He made no complaint to the rector, but on the following Sunday, when he retired to the vestry, thq clerk sat in stony silence. In a few mometits when he returned and climbed the steps, the clerk jumped from his seat, closed the pulpit door with a bang and cried out in a wild exultant tone, " We've got him, my boys; now let us sing to the praise and glory of God," etc. A plod story of the had old days (which would be equally unbelievable if it were not vouched for) belongs to Radcliffe, Lancashire. The clerk was given ,to sporting, and was secretary of the races committee. A meeting had been arranged for a Wednesday after an evening service, and the vicar decided to keep the clerk away from the meeting by preaching a very long sermon. There were not many present and therefore, not many people to suffer. Half an hour passed and the clerk became restless. Another half-hour dragged on and still the parson talked. It was too late for the race committee, and the clerk was thoroughly angry. When the sermon dragged to a close the clerk got up quiveriAg

===== 0002601_19150828_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
MOUNFIELD.

for his revenge. " Lot us sing," he said, "the 'undred and nineteenth psalm from yend to yend. He's preached all day and we'll sing aw neet."

WHEN THE CLERK MADE ANNOUNCE- MENTS.

Of course, all the sporting stories do n9t centre in the clerk. The parsons of a century ago—and less—followed the hounds as eagerly as any of their parishioners. A clerk was asked by the rector to announce that there would be no service on Ash Wednesday. In his glee at the prospect he made the state- ment as follows: "There will be no service on Wednesday—'coss why? Mester be goin' huntin' and so beeze I."

A young rector of a sporting turn explained to his clerk that ho would like him to an- nounce that there would be no afternoon ser- vice, adding, for his private . ear, that he wanted to get away to be in time for the races the day after. The clerk broke the news to the people: "This is to give notice, no avviss here this afternoon, becos measter meyans to got to Worthing to-night to be in good toime for reayces to-Morror mornin'." These parsons deserved to suffer, but some- times we have to put our sympathy on their side. Thus a (clerk announced that there would be no service " becos parson's goin' fishin' in the next parish." A member of the congregation remon- strated. " Parson told I sol hisself." "But he surely said officiating—not fishing." The clerk was not convinced. "If the apostles were fishermen," ho said, " Why couldn't their parson be one."

===== 0002601_19150828_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RECTOR'S FALSE TEETH.

We cannot refrain from another story of a church announcernynti, told by Cuthbert Bede. The old rector of a remote village had sent his false teeth to a neighbouring town for repairs, having received a firm promise that they should be returned in time for Sunday. The dentist failed him, and the old man had to face the congregation and do his best. After mumbling the prayers, he decided that it would be impossible for him to preach, and he took the clerk into the vestry ,to ask him to make a nice little apology to the congrega- tion. The rector from his place of retreat heard the following: "This is to give notice! As there won't be no sarmon, nor no more service this mornin', so you'd better all go whum; and there won't be no sarvice this afternoon, as the rector ain't got his artful teeth back from the dentist."

A midwinter notice was once given out as follows: "41 'ereby give notice that there will be no sarvice in this 'ere church this adernoon. 'Cos why? 'Cos the lamps be friz." More trying to the facial control of the congregation was a solemn statement made in a Devonshire church. A small organ had been bought,, but it was found that no music could be got out of it that morning. The clerk rose in his place. "Ladies and gentle- men, this is to give notice that the orgin wont play to-day, as he's burst his belly."

===== 0002601_19150828_art0236.txt | included=true | included =====
THEY FORGOT GOOD FRIDAY.

Yet another church announcement, in some respect the most wonderful of all. On an Easter Sunday the clerk stood up in a Shrop- shire village church and said: "This is notice that last Friday was Good Friday, but everybody forgot all about it, so we'll have next Friday instead." A village that could forget all about Good Friday—what a place for tired nerves and the rest cure!

Of altercations between the parson and clerk there are many stories, few of them that we like to recall. In a Yorkshire parish there was a clerk very fond of his pipe. Out of this arose a grievance against the State which took a curious form. The clergyman read the versicle "0 Lord, save the King," but there was no reply. He read it again, but without response. Thinking the clerk had fallen asleep he leaned over the pulpit and shook him. Then came a voice, "A we'ant ! Not till he taks tax off 'bacca."

===== 0002601_19150828_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
OTHER DAYS, OTHER WAYS.

A few other incidents will show us how far we have travelled in less than a hundred years.

A clergyman was preaching on a 'winter afternoon. The light was failing, and his manuscript gave him trouble. He came upon a sentence like this, "Enthusiasm, my brethren, in a good cause in excellent, but unless it is tempered with judgment it is apt to lead us—to lead us— Here, Thomas," reaching down with the sermon, " go to the window and see what it is apt to lead us into." The clerk of Strathfieldsaye once startled a strange clergyman who did not fully know the habits of the parish. The choir sat in the gallery and the numbers were few •" Mon I 'elp them chaps? They be terrible few," the clerk said to him. The clergyman agreed. After the clerk had reached the gallery a mdn came in late, and the parson almost collapsed with fright as he saw the clerk rise and throw a book at his head. The 111/th had knelt to say his private prayer, and the clerk aimed at him With deadly effect. The minister was dis- turbed by the incident, and asked for an ex- planation afterwards. The clerk showed no concern. "Oh,", he said, "it were only my way o' tellin' him to get up and sing, as we were terrible short this marning." A Lancashire clerk had a dreadful habit, from which nothing could wean him, of mak- ing comments upon the lesson. After reading the story of the murder of Sisera by Jael, the wife of Heber, he was evidently deeply stirred. In, a voice that could be heard through the church he said savagely: " Bad lot them wimen ! Bad, all't lot of 'em."

===== 0002601_19150828_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
0 CRIKEY!

The clerk of Woburn was also town crier. He ,gave out the hymns in his official voice from. the west gallery. The vicar tells that one Sunday morning lie was shocked .at hear- ing a loud voice break out "0 yes! 0 yes! 0 yes! This is to give notice." Then dis- covering that he was in Church he shouted, "0 crikey !—let us sing to the praise and glory of God." Those were days indeed, and ministers who tried to improve matters had much to endure. A new vicar appointed to a church eight miles from Oxford, in his desire to improve the congregation, offered to give sixpence a Sunday to all who attended church. The plan appeared to .work well, but only for a short time. He consulted the clerk and asked for his. opinion as to the reason of the falling off. " Well, sir," said the clerk, " it is like this: they tells me as how they finds it can't be endured for the money." Another clergyman who was genuinely anxious to impress the people and Who seemed utterly to fail, took the clerk aside and asked him whether he had understood any of his sermons, and whether he could help him by making any further explanations. The clerk replied that there was a sermon he had been thinking about, on and off, for weeks. Inquiry showed , that the sermon was on the destruction of the herd of swine, Mark v. 1-17.

The preachei was delighted at having made an impression, but his happiness was short lived. "Yes," said the clerk, in solemn and puzzled tone, " ah've bin wunnerin' ever since who paid for them pigs."

===== 0002601_19150828_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
MUSIC AND HEALING.

inf;•rnlary Sunday Passes Off Successfully.

LAST YEAR'S FIGURES BEATEN.

Warrington had a musical day on Sunday, under the auspices of the . orking Men's Infirmary Committee, to whom a united vote of thanks is due from all those lovers of good music who spent delightful hours in the Parr Hall, and later in the Hippodrome. The first part of the proceedings consisted of a render- ing of Handel's oratorio, "The Messiah," by a choir, of two hundred and fifty voices drawn from Churches of all denominations in the town, accompanied by an orchestra of local instrumentalists. This performance started in the Park, but after about half an hour the rain, which had commenced playfully to spray the crowd, who merely put up their umbrellas and serenely ignored the obnoxious efforts of the meteorological hosepipe, proved too much for the effectiveness of the manipulation of the stringed instruments, and an adjournment was made to the Tarr Hall, where the oratorio was finished. The evening was given over to a per- formance by the band of the 2nd South Lancs. Regiment. For both entertainments there were crowded audiences, on whom the excellence of the music, vocal and instrumental, was not lost. The proceeds of the festival, totalling £67 19s. 914., are being devoted to the Warring- ton Infirmary and to the fund for providing comforts for the soldiers and sailors operating at the Dardanelles.

In the afternoon the sum of 2,30 7s. Md. was collected, and in the evening £37 12s. 6d. The grand total exceeds that of last year by a few pence short of £7. The afternoon's performance was thoroughly enjoyed and went admirably, despite the in- terruption of the weather, which damped no one's spirits, no matter what it did to their raiment, and which was neatly described by Mr. George Walker, in announcing the resump- tion, as "an interval for refreshments." . The programme included two hymns, "0 God, our help in ages past," with which the festival opened, and John Oxenham's fine hymn for the men at the Front. The former; sung in the open air, sounded impressive, but it was surpassed by the solemn grandeur of the people's prayer for their warrior countrymen and for a lasting peace. Sung by a crowded audience to the tune of "Eternal Father, strong to save," the effect was magnificent

===== 0002601_19150828_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE MESSIAH."

"The Messiah," as performed by the princi- pals, chorus and orchestra, under Mr. Payton's baton, was, of course, a much curtailed version of Handel's great masterpiece, only the more animated aria 4 and the more massive choruses, along with the overture and the pastoral sym- phony, being attempted. Mr. W. Booth was fortunate in being able to declaim the recitative "Comfort ye" and to sing the spirited aria "Every valley" before the rain came on, and in these two numbers he sang with a praise- worthy earnestness and naturalness which en- abled him to give them due effect. By the time, however, that Mr. Pearce had given us "Thus saith the Lord" and "Who may abide" it was clearly seen that the weather was about to break down, an anti-climax that was most unfortunately realised during Madame Arm- strong's singing of the solo, with chorus, "0 Thou that tellest," in which her fine voice and firm delivery were, of course, to a large extent lost in the collapsing of the stringed instru- ments in a sudden shower of rain. At this point it was decided to transfer everything and every- body to the Parr Hall, an operation which can- not have sacrificed less than half an hour of precious time. Here a much vaster audience assembled than was gathered in the Park, the hall being filled in every part. Resuming the programme of items Mr. W. H. Rigby sang the recitative and aria commencing "For behold darkness," and although handicapped by sur- rounding instruments not yet quite attuned after their damping, he interpreted these some- what doleful numbers in a manly voice and in a manner which did infinite credit to his reputa- tion as a rising vocalist. Miss Elsie Hewitt had only a few bars to sing in the recitative "There were shepherds," but the bell-like tones of her voice and her clear enunciation of the words. , made quite a supcess of this important passage. The florid air "Rejoice" . essayed by Madame Sara Silcock was in every way suited to her brilliant and flexiMe soprano voice and the charming naivete of her style and deportment. Needless to say, she was eminently successful ' in realising both the cheery sentiment of the , words and the exuberant joyousness of the music. Mrs. Sanders and Miss May Hockenhall shared between them the honours of the twin. arias "He shall feed His flock" and "Come unto Him," the first in the full-toned contralto which is pleasurably well known to the War- rington music-loving public, and the second in a brilliant soprano voiceof quite startling purity and penetrating power. • With the careful avoid- ance of a tendency to stridency in the produc- tion of her voice, this young singer has every prospect of a successful future in the exercise and development of her vocal gifts. Mr. Goostrey's rendering of "Thou didst not leave" was too slow and lacking in animation to engage the full attention of the audience, albeit he sang this flowing aria`in a tenor voice of a pleasant quality Produced quite naturally and without manifest effort. P•obably the very best thing of its kind was the singing by Mr. E. Pearce of the dramatic air, "Why do the Nations?"—his powerful bass voice and robust style proving just the equipment for rightly • interpreting this telling solo. Mr. Pearce came in .for the lion's share of the un- stinted applause bestowed by the audience on , the various principals. The choruses were, on the whole, intelligently and painstakingly rendered, and occasionally, as in "Lift un your heads" and the "Halle- lujah Chorus" a fine body of vocal tone Was produced. The orchestra, too, acquitted them- selves excellently, having regard to the dis- abilities under which the string playerg worked- in the damp of out-of-door conditions. The pastoral symphony was particularly well played and gave evident pleasure to the audi- ence. It should be noted that as leader of the orchestra Mr. T. Bradford Barton did good service, as did. also Mr. H. L. Read at the harmonium and piano. . In an interval, the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) briefly addressed the audience, thank- ing, on behalf of the committee; all who had contributed to the success of the festival; and appealing to those who could do so further to help the cause by attending the Hippodrome in the evening.

===== 0002601_19150828_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
SOUTH LANCASHIRE BAND AT THE

lIIPPOMIOME.

Brilliant Performance.

A first-rate entertainment was given in the Hippodrome (courteously granted by Mr. F. Macnaghten) in the evening by the Band of the 2nd South Lancashire Regiment (by kind per- mission of Colonel J. A. Moggridge) and two local singers. There was a full and appreciative audi- ence. Under the skilful conductorship of Mr. R. E. Cahill, the band discoursed a number of pieces with the precision and crispness of attack and the brilliance of intonation which we have become accustomed to associate with all the per- formances of this most capable body of instru- mentalists. Mr. Percy Fletcher's quite recently composed march "The Spirit of Pageantry," played with befitting animation, formed an ap- propriate interlude to some more extended pieces that were to follow. The two most im- portant of these were the "Gipsy Life" suite of Le Thiere and the fantasia on themes taken from Verdi's numerous operas. The suite had, perhaps, the more intrinsic interest, as its movements being more closely knit, there was about it none of ,that feeling of scrappiness which is inevitable in the stringing together of unconnected melodies, some of which have not even the merit of strong contrast. "Gipsy Life" aroused such interest that there followed an enthusiastic demand for further favours, to which response was made in the delightful play- ing of the now well-known Barcarolle from the "Tales of Hoffman" (Offenbach). The "Ballet Egyptienne " (Luigini), by vividly realising a characteristically Oriental flavour, at once riveted attention, and deserves a special meed of praise for the excellent way in which it was presented. The cornet solo, "Softly awakes my heart," from Saint Saens's opera, "Samson and Delilah," was performed by Bandsman Horace Swift with artistic skill and finish, and well

merited the vociferous applause bestowed upon it. Madame Sara Silcock achieved a great success in Roeckel's song "Angus Macdonald," the dramatic and descriptive 'portions of which ,were well brought out both by the vocalist and the bandsmen, for whom the accompaniments had evidently been specially scored. Earning a well-merited encore, Madame Silcock responded by singing "The Rosary," also with band accompani- ment, which greatly enhanced its effect. The song "When you come home,' by Mr. W. H. Rigby, was sung by him in quite a musicianly manner, but it hardy seemed suited to his voice and style, which demands some- thing more essentially masculine in character. Nevertheless he so far succeeded in effectively interpreting it as to receive an unmistakable recall, to whic.h he made suitable response.

THANKS TO THE ARTISTES.

Lady Greenall And An Excellent Musical

===== 0002601_19150828_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
Treat.

The Mayor and Lady Greenall, in an inter- val, appeared on the stage, amidst hearty ap- plause, and His Worship announced that he had asked Lady Greenall to speak a few words of thanks on behalf of the committee. He added that the total takings for the evening amounted to .237 12s. 6d., including a cheque from Lady Greenall. (Applause.) Lady Greenall, who was loudly applauded, said it was her very pleasant duty to thank Colonel Moggridge for giving permission to the band to appear_ to provide such an excellent musical treat. Special thanks were due to Mr. Cahill and the Members of the band for their voluntary services and to other kind friends who had rendered assistance. The services of the band were highly appreciated, particularly when the fact was taken into consideration that the majority of the players had been to the Front, had been wounded, and had been invalided home. She believed she was correct when she stated that two of the men were recipients of the D.C.M. (Applause.) Thanks were due to Mr. Maenaghten, who was always willing to render assistance to a charitable cause, to the management and staff for their voluntary services, to Madame Silcock, Mr. Rigby and to the audience for attending in such large numbers. It was our duty to pro- vide comforts for our brave soldiers, whether they were in hospital at home or were fighting at the Front, daily, and hourly offering their lives for the sake of England and their loved ones at home. While thanking the audience for their attendance, she had' no hesitation in asking for their continued support to local charities, which the people of Warrington were always so willing and anxious to give. (Ap- plause.) The band played "God bless the Prince of Wales," and the audience heartily joined in singing the first verse of the National Anthera.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

NEW IRISH PLAY AT THE ROYAL COURT.

The Irish play that is being presented by Mr. and Mrs. Chalmers Mackey and their efficient company at the Royal Court Theatre this week., is based on an Irish history and the ploftings of a tYrannical "beggar on horseback" type of landlord and a Satanic accomplice to bring home to a loyal son of the people the guilt of an offence punishable, with a long exile, provide the dramatic theme. There is no lack of tun in ,the piece, which is well presente4,, both in the histrionic and scenic meaning. The setting in the final scene but one, in which, at mid- night, the upstart landlord throws his cunning and cruel old catspaw over the bridge, is excep- tionally effective. Mr. Chalmers Mackey is the life of the piece. His portrayal of the character of Patsey O'Toole, a cheery, good-hearted young Irish- man, affecting to be possessed of much cynical wisdom, but sorely susceptible to the wiles of schamin' wimmen," is a good bit of work. He looks the part and acts with great natural- ness. With Mr. Mackey is associated, in some amusing passages, Miss Nana Davey, as Biddy Brady. The double part of Shaun O'Connor and Dennis O'Connor is ably taken by Mr. Dennis Mackey. Mrs. Chalmers Mackey. as , Mary O'Conor; Mr. Frank Dalton, as Jerry Grogan, the agent; Mr. Chas. Julian, as Squire 10'Hagon; Mr. Seymour Rose and Mr. Wm. ,Vane, as counsel; and Miss Maud Lilian, as the charming Helen Travers; Miss 'Jeanette Lyons, as Widow McGrath; and Mr. St. John Stuart, as Father Dan Rearden, all contribute capably to the audience's enjoyment of "The Soggarth Aroon."
