GOOD ANIMAL " TURN " AT THE

HIPPODROME.

Cats and dogs and a pony provide an excellent tarn' at the Hippodrome this 'week. ' Tho cats are the " star " feature of the show, their accomplishments affording great entertainment to thp large audiences. The animals are the property of Silbon, .who unites with one-of his feline colleagues in an act described as "loop- ing the human loop." Seever's Imperial Russian Troupe, a pictur- esque lot, dance with skill and rim, and add ta their popularity with ,the audience by con- cluding with the National Anthems pf the Allies.

Betty Boden gives a delightful act. She is a clever mimic, a graceful dancer, and has a good voice. Her " turn " is one of the most popular in the programme.

A clever trick pianist is Will H. Fox, who does several unusual things, for instance, play- ing the -instrument through a cloth placed over the keys, and with his eyes blindfolded, and also playing a tune with his olfactory member. Rob, Ivy and Lyn are a gifted trio, and con- tribute in no small measure to the pleasure of the audience with their musical scene, "The Garden of Music." Lomax, the man who has only one leg, but can hop further than most people can jump, gives a surprising act, and Albert Le Pro suc- ceeds in tickling the audience considerably. Sensation and Mystery Next Week.

Next week's all-star company includes the Four Sensdtional Boises,-" human aeroplanes"; Thora, mysterious ventriloquist; Lauri Bros.. ideal athletes; Sara, Williams, popular prima- donna; Lauri Howard, eccentric dancing; Barnard and Bunny, novelty .„comedians (in- cluding the human cat); and Zellini, comedy juggler.

DETECTIVE DRAMA AT THE STAR KINEMA.

To-day's films at the all-popular Star Kinema should attract large houses, for they are of high calibre—the chief one being "Alias Jimmy Valentine," a fine detective drama featuring Robert Warwick. . During the first week-half the premier film was a photo version 9f Elinor Glyn's famous "Three Weeks." A story so admirable might be expected to lose much when shown on the screen, but a powerful company and unstinted staging expenditure reduced the " loss " to a minimum. The film has provoked discussion through the town. "With His Hands" was another capital drama of how a lady worker saved her employer from a terrible death plotted by a malevolent workman. " Pathes' Animated Gazette" shows more life with the Indian Army and the Russian Navy—affording a particularly fine example of modern sea war- fare. The "Roll a Honour " ,continues as at- tractive as ever, as also do the humorous alms of Chaplin fame. " If I Were King "—Next Week.

During the first half of next week a film ver- sion of McCarthy's famous novel, "If I were King," will be presented; whilst "Two Women" promises well for the second three days.

His Worship the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), chairman of the Executive Committee, on their behalf, begs to remind the Public that the PARR HALL is NOW OPEN between the hours of 10 a.rn. and 4 p.m. to receive their GIFTS to the above funds in the shape of Furniture, Pictures, Curios, Useful and Ornamental Articles, or any- thing that could be sold by auction and converted into cash; of whioh there is imperative need. RUNCORN UNION. ASSISTANT NURSE. An Assistant Nurse (to. take alternative day and night duty) is required at the Workhouse at Dutton, near Warrington. • Salary, ..t25 per annum,' with uniform, board, washing, and lodgings in the Nurses' Home within the Workhouse precincts. Candidates must have received some 4aining in general nursing, and the appointment.will le subject to the approval of the Local Government Board. Applications, stating age, experience, and whether single or widow, accompanied by copies of not more than two recent testimonials, must reach my Office, 71, High-street, Runcorn, not later than Ten o'clock on Monday morning, the 30th day of August, 1915. Any further information may be obtained from the Matron of the Workhouse. W. E. HOUGH, Acting Clerk to the Guardians. Union Offices, Runcorn, 19th August, 1915.

Mr. RAcKerma.,Chancellor of the Exchequer,says:— ~ The man, he he rich or poor, is little to be envied who, at this supreme moment, fails to bring forward his savings for the security of his country." y Ake' W ,çp invest in the an, • You can still take your share in the great 41- X War Loan through the Post Office. If you have saved Z 5 or more, go to the Post Office and buy of Scrip Certificates or as many of them as you can. These Certifi- cates will be exchanged in any Money Order Office between Ist and 15th December, 1915, , for the same amount of 4-1% War Loan Stock. If you exchange your Certificates for Stock you will get interest at the rate of 5% per annum, or sd. per month for each £5 Certificate from the first clay of the month after you bought the Cei-tificate until 30th November; you will also get a bonus of 1/- for each £5 Certificate that you so exchange into Stock. After Ist December your Stock will bear interest at the rate of 41-% per annum, that is to say that each half year you will get 2s. 3d. for each £5 so invested. If you have only a few shillings handy go to the Post Office and buy a War Loan Scrip Voucher. You can have either 55., 10s., or Z 1 vouchers and you can buy as many as you like. Keep them carefully until Ist December when you can exchange each twenty ss. vouchers for a £5 Stock Certificate. You will also receive the interest your money has earned up to that time, and in addition a bonus of is. for each -.C5 of Stock that you take. Afterwards, as long as you own your Stock, you will receive your interest on June Ist and December Ist each year. Remember vou can at any time sell your War Loan Stock at the market price through the Post Office. If you cannot see your way to collect enough vouchers to buy Z 5 worth of Stock within the next year, do not buy vouchers but put your money into the Post Office Sayings Bank. IN FRONT AT THE FRONT. Pleasure Given By " Examiner " Beyond Description. night Warrington men in one. Battery ia France have written to us this week to let US know that they all enjoy reading "The Examiner." Says the actual writer of the letter which is signed by the eight:— " I or one of my chums have it sent out regularly every week, and the pleasure we derive from it is beyond description, as naturally we love to hear a bit of news about the dear old place, and also to hold the paper • • in our hands once again, the paper which we were all so used to reading at home. "There are quite a number of Warring- ton ' boys ' in this Battery, and when the paper arrives there is quite a scramble for the first look at it, I can assure you. Are YOU giving your friends this great pleasure by sending them "The Examiner :'?

TERRITORIAL ROLL OF HONOUR. P. !MATE KILLED BY A FALLING WALL. The lull at the Front is still shown in the absence of casualties among our Territorials. As will be seen by a letter from Sergeant Widocks, which we publish to-day, our men have been engaged on "more important" work than work in the trenches. KILLED. Private William Smith. TOTAL CASUALTIES. The total casualties in the Battalion now are : Killed Missing Wounded Total PRIVATE WILLIAM SMITH. Killed At The Front By Fall Of A Wall. While demolishing some old property at the Front, along with several of his comrades of

home arrived the day after his death, and in it his concluding words are Bang! There goes a 'Jack Johnson.' I shall•have to close now!" Beside* his widow, Private Wilcock leaves two young sons and a , daughter. His two other brothers, now serving with the Colours, are Lieutenant James Wilcock, 10th South Lancs., and Corporal Frank Wilcock, of the Warrington Territorials. From youth these two patriots have been Volunteers. HOWARD DAVIS. Ex-Warrington Football Player's Patriotism. Sergeant Howard Davis, who in July, 1913, signed on for the Warrington Football Club, OH the disbandonment of Coventry, was killed in action, July 31st. On the outbreak of war Davis enlisted and joined the Bth Battalion Rifle Brigade, in which he soon rose to the rank of Sergeant. He was 27 years of age and, during his short connection with the local club, had many admirers. He played three-quarter and, without shining particularly, did some very valuable work for the team. A THOROUGH ATHLETE. Sergeant Davis, who was 6ft. high and weighed 12st. 31b, when he came to Warring- ton, was an all-round athlete, being a fine runner, high jump and fast bowler at cricket. He. however, was most prominent as a foot- baller. lie began to play Rugby football ttt the age of ten, on jeining King Edward's Grammar School, Aston, Birmingham. and I was told that he had been hit in the arm by a bomb. They said he was not badly hurt, so it has grieved me very much•to hear from you that he had died. I have been .expecting a letter from him thinking that he would be in Warrington'with you by now. Suffered From Gas. "John was a bright and cheery lad, and was aly.-ays liked by everyone; he was about to be promoted when he came out of the trenches. "He was in hospital early in May suffering from gas poisoning, and he rejoined about three weeks when he was wounded. You have my deepest symppthy as well as that of his other comrades, for I know John was a good lad when at home. I do hope you r will 'try and console yourself with the knowledge that he has died a , hero's death, and that he received his fatal I wounds at a spot where many more have laid ' down their lives. I know it is hard, but I hope you will try to bear it bravely, If I am more fortunate and live to come' home-to Warrington I will come to see you." RIFLEMAN H. LANGLEY. Dies. ST. GEORGE'S MEDAL. Private Henry Brookes •Cains Czar's High Reward. • CREAT HONOUR TO LYAIII. In the official honour lists published on Thurs- day morning appeared the .name of 1787 Private Henry Brookes, of the Earl of Chester's /sth Cheshire Territorials, as having been awarded the fourth class of the Russian Medal of St. George for heroic bravery in ambulance /work. There are four others awarded.by the Czar, but only one other of the Cheshires—a corporal in. the 3rd Battalion. Private Henry. Brookes has undoubtedly earried the great honour which he brings thus to his native village—an honour which must be accounted by far the greatest individual distinc-
